Effective transmission through an interface with evolving microstructure
Lucas M. Fix, Gianna Götzmann, Malte A. Peter, Jan-F. Pietschmann

Abstract
We study the asymptotic behaviour of a system of nonlinear reaction–diffusion–advection equations in a domain consisting of two bulk regions connected via microscopic channels distributed within a thin membrane. Both the width of the channels and the thickness of the membrane are of order ε ≪ 1 \varepsilon\ll 1 , and the geometry evolves in time in an a priori known way. We consider nonlinear flux boundary conditions at the lateral boundaries of the channels and critical scaling of the diffusion inside the layer. Extending the method of homogenisation in domains with evolving microstructure to thin layers, we employ two-scale convergence and unfolding techniques in thin layers to derive an effective model in the limit ε → 0 \varepsilon\to 0 , in which the membrane is reduced to a lower-dimensional interface. We obtain jump conditions for the solution and the total fluxes, which involve the solutions of local, space–time-dependent cell problems in the reference channel.



1
Introduction

Thin porous layers with microscopic heterogeneous structures which regulate exchange between two bulk regions play a fundamental role in a multitude of applications ranging from biological transport to industrial filtration processes. Examples include ion channels distributed within cell membranes
[
Hil01
]
, polymer separators in lithium-ion batteries
[
AZ04
]
, single-ion-conducting membranes in polymer–electrolyte fuel cells
[
WN04
]
and thin-film composite membranes used in reverse osmosis
[
Bak12
,
Gei+10
]
.
In these systems, macroscopic transport properties, like permeability, selectivity and effective reaction rates, are determined by the microscopic geometry of the layer, which exhibits spatial heterogeneity at scales several orders of magnitude smaller than the bulk regions.

Additional complexity arises when the microscopic geometry undergoes an evolution in time. For example, this is the case in conformational changes of ion channels regulating their conductivity
[
Hil01
,
Lóp+19
]
, dendrite growth progressively occluding pores in battery separators
[
JEG15
]
or pore blocking or fouling phenomena altering the effective pore size distribution in membrane filtration
[
Men+09
]
. Consequently, transport processes are coupled to geometric changes, leading to time-dependent effective properties which must be accounted for in predictive models.

Direct numerical simulations of such systems face a fundamental challenge as they require to resolve both the macroscopic (observation) scale and the microscopic scale of the heterogeneities. This motivates the combined use of techniques from periodic homogenisation and dimension reduction to derive effective models encoding the effect of the microstructure in upscaled non-trivial transmission conditions given at a sharp interface between the bulk regions, thus allowing for a macroscopic approximation of the fully resolved system.

In this work, we apply this strategy to a prototypical system of nonlinear reaction–diffusion–advection equations for an unknown vector of concentrations
u_{\varepsilon}=u_{\varepsilon}(t,x)
in a microscopic domain composed of two bulk regions
\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}
(in which
u_{\varepsilon}
is denoted by
u_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}
) connected through channels in a thin porous layer
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)
of thickness
\varepsilon>0
(in which
u_{\varepsilon}
is denoted by
u_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
), where the time parameter
t
describes the current configuration of the time-evolving (not necessarily periodic) heterogeneous microstructure. The microscopic system under consideration reads

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\ }c@{\ \, }l}\partial_{t}u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}-\nabla\cdot\left(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}-u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm}\right)&=&f_{j}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})&&\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm},\\
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}-\nabla\cdot\left(\varepsilon D_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}-u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}q_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\right)&=&\frac{1}{\varepsilon}g_{j}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})&&\text{in }\left\{(t,x)\,\colon\,t\in(0,T),\,x\in\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)\right\},\\
(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}-u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm})\cdot\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times(\partial\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\setminus S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}),\\
-(\varepsilon D_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}-u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}q_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}b_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})\cdot\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}&=&h_{j}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})&&\text{on }\left\{(t,x)\,\colon\,t\in(0,T),\,x\in N_{\varepsilon}(t)\right\},\end{array}\right.

where
b_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
is the velocity of the evolving lateral boundary
N_{\varepsilon}(t)
of the channels. Moreover, we equip the system with appropriate transmission conditions on the top and bottom
S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
of the channels, guaranteeing the continuity of the solution and the total fluxes.
The diffusion in the thin layer is of critical order
\varepsilon
, i.e. the time scale of diffusion in the channels with respect to the microscopic scale matches that of diffusion in the bulk on the macroscopic scale, see
[
PB05
]
, and the (surface) reaction kinetics are assumed to be nonlinear with Lipschitz growth. The goal of this work is to establish a rigorous combined homogenisation–dimension reduction analysis as
\varepsilon\to 0
of the problem above including the identification of the system of equations satisfied by the limit of
u_{\varepsilon}
.

In a prototypical application,
u_{\varepsilon}
would model the concentration of a chemical substance (i.e. in terms of its volume fraction) as it is transported through the channels by means of diffusion and drift due to the velocity field
q_{j\varepsilon}
. In this setting, the terms
f_{j}
and
g_{j}
would account for reactions in the respective part of the domain, while
h_{j}
describes the transfer of mass through the channel walls. One concrete example for the latter phenomenon is the motion of charged ions through ion channels
[
SNE01
]
or artificial nanopores in polymers
[
Pie+13
]
. There, the change of the channel geometry could model an opening and closing (ion channels) or molecules binding and unbinding to the channel walls (nanopores). Clearly, a prescribed evolution only serves as a first step towards the more realistic situation of a coupling to the actual concentrations as well as the mass flux through the channel walls, which presents an interesting venue for further investigations.

Mathematical Approach and Contributions

In what follows,
\varepsilon>0
is a small parameter describing the typical length of the microscopic channels in relation to the observation length scale as well as the periodicity of the fixed reference layer
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
which is built by periodic repetition of the
\varepsilon
-scaled unit channel cell
Z_{*}
. We assume the evolution of the channels to be given a priori in terms of a map
\psi_{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}\to{\mathbbm{R}}^{n}
, that is
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)=\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})
, and similarly for the moving boundary
N_{\varepsilon}(t)
of the channels. The maps
\psi_{\varepsilon}
are assumed to be
C^{1}
-diffeomorphisms satisfying particular
\varepsilon
-uniform estimates with so-called limit transformation
\psi_{0}
, see
(T1)
–
(T4)
below.

Since the evolution of the microstructure is known a priori, we follow the approach outlined in
[
Pet07
]
and transform the problem to one on the fixed reference geometry
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
, but with coefficients dependent on the transformation
\psi_{\varepsilon}
itself. Subsequently, we provide a rigorous derivation of the macroscopic problem making use of the theory of two-scale convergence and unfolding techniques in thin domains, see
[
NJ07
,
MM00
,
GN21
,
BGN22
]
. Their application is based on
\varepsilon
-uniform a priori estimates for the solutions in Sobolev–Bochner spaces with
\varepsilon
-dependent weights adapted to the problem-structure. Moreover,
\varepsilon
-uniform estimates only hold for the transformed time derivatives
\partial_{t}(J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon})
, where
J_{\varepsilon}=\det\mathrm{D}\psi_{\varepsilon}
denotes the Jacobian of the transformation, and not for
\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}
. As these estimates scale badly in
\varepsilon
when restricted to the layer
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
, a standard Aubin–Lions-type argument cannot be applied to obtain strong convergence of the microsolutions. Instead, we use unfolding techniques in thin domains which require the application of a Kolmogorov–Simon-type argument. The latter one is based on an abstract duality argument for obtaining the existence and
\varepsilon
-uniform estimates of the time derivative of the unfolded sequence, and the derivation of estimates on shifts of the microsolutions.
Finally, an abstract convergence result for the generalised time derivatives is needed in order to pass to the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
, where we obtain two reaction–diffusion–advection-type problems posed on the bulk domains
\Omega^{\pm}
separated by the sharp interface
\Sigma
, and coupled by effective transmission conditions for the solutions and the total fluxes on
\Sigma
. These transmission conditions are given in terms of local cell problems posed in the channel cell
Z_{*}
.
Here, the macrovariable
x^{\prime}\in\Sigma
enters as a parameter as we consider the critical case of small diffusivity of order
\varepsilon
in the layer, leading to rapid oscillations of the microsolutions with respect to the spatial variable, and hence a dependency on the macro- and the microvariable of the local cell solutions in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
. After applying the reverse transformation (which is given in terms of the two-scale limit
\psi_{0}
of the microscopic transformations
\psi_{\varepsilon}
) we eventually obtain local cell problems, each posed on an evolving microcell
Z_{*}(t,x^{\prime})
, and thus capturing the limiting evolution of the microstructure.

To the best of our knowledge, this is the first time that the techniques of rigorous dimension reduction and homogenisation in a thin layer, whose microstructure evolves with time, are combined. Our main results are as follows.

•

Derivation of
\varepsilon
-uniform a priori estimates for the microsolutions and their shifts on the fixed reference domain (including the thin layer
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
), taking into account the transformation-dependent coefficients (see
Lemma 3.4
and
Lemma 3.6
).

•

General compactness and convergence result for the generalised time derivatives considered as functionals on the whole microscopic domain
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
(see
Theorem 4.3
).

•

Rigorous dimension reduction and homogenisation limit, both on the fixed reference and the evolving domain. This includes the derivation and analysis of a macroscopic problem of reaction–diffusion–advection-type, subject to effective transmission conditions across the interface
\Sigma
, which are given in terms of local cell problems on the evolving cell
Z_{*}(t,x^{\prime})
(see
Theorem 4.8
and
Corollary 4.16
).

The results and framework established here provide a basis for the more complex scenario in which the channel evolution is fully coupled to the reaction–diffusion–advection process.

Literature

An approach to analyse the homogenisation of problems posed on domains with time-dependent microstructure by transforming them onto a fixed reference domain was proposed in
[
Pet07
,
Mei08
]
and applied to coupled reaction–diffusion processes in
[
Pet09
]
and chemical degradation mechanisms in
[
Pet07a
,
PB09
]
. By a similar technique, surface exchange processes and chemical reactions at interfaces were treated in
[
Dob15
]
. The aforementioned strategy assumes the equivalence of the direct homogenisation procedure on the time-dependent domain and the one on the fixed reference geometry, which which was proven for porous bulk domains by
[
Wie22
]
.
This approach allows the application of two-scale convergence and unfolding methods (see
[
All92
,
Ngu89
,
CDG02
]
and the classical textbooks
[
Hor97
,
CS99
,
CDG18
]
) also in the case of a priori time-dependent geometries. Based on this method, in
[
GNP21
]
rigorous homogenisation results for reaction–diffusion–advection equations in bulk domains with a priori known evolution of the microstructure were established.
The corresponding analysis for the quasistationary Stokes flow through perforated media was performed in
[
WP24
]
, and in
[
WP25
]
for the instationary case, including the identification of memory effects arising from the evolution.
A linear, fully coupled thermoelasticity problem in a two-phase medium was studied in
[
EM17
]
, demonstrating that similar techniques extend to systems undergoing phase transformations.

If the evolution of the microstructure is an unknown of the problem itself and coupled to the transport processes, the analysis becomes even more challenging. For mineral dissolution and precipitation models, we mention
[
GP23
]
, where the local concentration determines the (radially symmetric) evolution of the grains. The homogensiation analysis of the closely related problem local colloid evolution induced by reaction and diffusion was performed in
[
WP23
]
. Very recently, a comprehensive treatment of Stokes flow, advection–reaction–diffusion and adsorption–desorption processes in freely evolving porous media, i.e. fully coupled to the evolution of microstructure of the medium, has been presented in
[
Gah+25
]
, which additionally provides a detailed overview of the state of the art in the field of homogenisation of evolving microstructures. We also mention
[
EM26
]
, in which a one-phase thermoelasticity system with phase transformations and small growing or shrinking inclusions was studied, describing the evolution by a height function and applying the Hanzawa transform.

The derivation of effective interface and transmission conditions for transport between bulk regions across thin layers combines homogenisation with dimension reduction.
For fixed heterogeneous layers, rigorous transmission conditions for reaction–diffusion processes with critical diffusivity of order
\varepsilon
were derived in
[
NJ07
]
. Effective transmission coefficients determined by local cell problems were obtained, thus incorporating information on the microstructure in the macroscopic model. This framework was extended to diffusivities of order
\varepsilon^{\gamma}
,
\gamma\in[-1,1)
, in
[
GNK17
]
, leading to qualitatively different interface conditions, and to nonlinear transmission conditions at the bulk–layer interface in
[
GNK18
]
. Corrector and rigorous error estimates were established in
[
GJN21
]
, providing quantitative bounds on the approximation error.
For thin layers composed of periodic channel-like structures, in
[
GN21
]
the case of critically scaled diffusivity was analysed, including the derivation of effective jump conditions given in terms of local cell problems in the reference channel. These results were extended in
[
BGN22
]
to more general reaction kinetics at the lateral boundaries and subcritical scaling.
By means of gradient flow theory and EDP-convergence, diffusion–advection in a structure composed of thin layers whose thickness is tending to zero at different rates was analysed in
[
FL21
]
, showing that the limit system also has a gradient structure.

Structure of the paper

This paper is organised as follows. In
Section 2
, we introduce the microscopic model, the geometric setting and the assumptions on the evolution of the microstructure as well as on the data. Then, in
Section 3
the problem on the time-dependent geometry is transformed to one on a fixed, periodic reference geometry. Next, existence of weak solutions in function spaces adapted to the scaling of the equations in the layer and uniform a priori estimates for the microsolutions and their shifts are established.
Section 4
deals with the derivation of the homogenised macroscopic problem, which is based on weak and strong two-scale compactness results for the microsolutions (
Subsection 4.1
), the limit passage
\varepsilon\to 0
and identification of the macroscopic problem on the reference geometry and local cell problems (
Subsection 4.2
) and finally the reverse transformation back to the evolving domain (
Subsection 4.3
). Necessary (known) results about two-scale convergence for thin channels are collected in
Appendix A
, while a summary of the notation used for function spaces and the description of the geometry is provided in the
List of symbols
.

Notation

Throughout this paper, we make use of the following notation.
We decompose coordinates
x\in{\mathbbm{R}}^{n}
into
x=(x^{\prime},x_{n})\in{\mathbbm{R}}^{n-1}\times{\mathbbm{R}}
. By
\lfloor x\rfloor
, we denote the floor function, applied in a component-wise manner to
x\in{\mathbbm{R}}^{n}
. Given an invertible matrix
F\in{\mathbbm{R}}^{n\times n}
, we define
F^{-\top}\coloneqq(F^{-1})^{\top}
.
For two
\varepsilon
-dependent quantities
A_{\varepsilon}
and
B_{\varepsilon}
, we write
A_{\varepsilon}\lesssim B_{\varepsilon}
if there exists a universal constant
C>0
independent of
\varepsilon
such that
A_{\varepsilon}\leq CB_{\varepsilon}
. Moreover, we write
A_{\varepsilon}\simeq B_{\varepsilon}
if
A_{\varepsilon}\lesssim B_{\varepsilon}
and
B_{\varepsilon}\lesssim A_{\varepsilon}
.
To shorten notation, we also set
\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits a_{\pm}=a_{+}+a_{-}
for
a_{\pm}\in{\mathbbm{R}}
.

We further use the following notation for function spaces.
For an open and bounded set
U\subset{\mathbbm{R}}^{n}
with Lipschitz boundary
\partial U
and
\Gamma\subset\partial U
we denote by
H^{1}_{\Gamma,0}(U)
the Sobolev space of functions in
H^{1}(U)
with vanishing trace on
\Gamma
.
For a Gelfand triple
V\xhookrightarrow{}H\xhookrightarrow{}V^{\prime}
, the Sobolev–Bochner space
W^{1,2,2}(0,T;V,V^{\prime})
consists of all functions in
L^{2}(0,T;V)
with generalised time derivative in
L^{2}(0,T;V^{\prime})
. A complete list of the function spaces used in this work is found in the
List of symbols
.

2
The microscopic model

We first give a precise description of the microscopic geometry evolving in time in terms of a (microscopic) reference layer. Then, we formulate the microscopic model and state the main assumptions regarding the time evolution of the microstructure and the data.

Description of the reference geometry

In what follows, let
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{k})_{k\in{\mathbbm{N}}}
be a sequence tending to
0
such that
\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k}}\in{\mathbbm{N}}
, and let
n\in{\mathbbm{N}}_{\geq 2}
and
H>0
be fixed.
We consider the macroscopic domain
\Omega\coloneqq Y\times(-H,H)\subset{\mathbbm{R}}^{n}
, where
Y\coloneqq(0,1)^{n-1}
is the
(n-1)
-dimensional unit cube, and we subdivide
\Omega
as follows. The bulk regions
\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{+}\coloneqq Y\times(\varepsilon,H)
and
\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{-}\coloneqq Y\times(-H,-\varepsilon)
are separated by the thin layer
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq Y\times(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
, and we denote the interfaces between these subsets by
S_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\coloneqq Y\times\{\pm\varepsilon\}
.
In the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
, the thin layer
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{\varepsilon}
reduces to the
(n-1)
-dimensional hypersurface
\Sigma\coloneqq Y\times\{0\}
and we write
\Omega^{+}=Y\times(0,H)
and
\Omega^{-}=Y\times(-H,0)
.

To describe the microscopic structure of the thin layer
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{\varepsilon}
, let
Z\coloneqq Y\times(-1,1)
be the
n
-dimensional standard unit cell and denote its upper and lower boundary by
S^{\pm}\coloneqq\left\{z\in\partial Z\,\colon\,z_{n}=\pm 1\right\}
.
A channel across
Z
is represented by an open Lipschitz domain
Z_{*}\subset Z
, see
2.1(a)
, for an illustration, such that both

\displaystyle S^{\pm}_{*}\coloneqq\left\{z\in\partial Z_{*}\,\colon\,z_{n}=\pm 1\right\}

(2.1)

are Lipschitz domains in
{\mathbbm{R}}^{n-1}
of positive measure. The lateral boundary of the channel is denoted by

\displaystyle N\coloneqq\partial Z_{*}\setminus(S^{+}_{*}\cup S^{-}_{*})

(2.2)

and we assume that
N
has a positive distance to
\partial Z\setminus(S^{+}\cup S^{-})
so that the lateral channel boundary does not touch the lateral boundary of the unit cell. The periodic microstructure of the thin layer
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{\varepsilon}
is then described by repetition of
\varepsilon Z_{*}
along
\Sigma
. More precisely, we define the channel domain
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
by

\displaystyle\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}\coloneqq\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}}\varepsilon(Z_{*}+(k^{\prime},0)),

(2.3)

where
{\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\left\{k^{\prime}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}^{n-1}\,\colon\,\varepsilon Z+\varepsilon(k^{\prime},0)\subset\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{\varepsilon}\right\}
, the combined interfaces
S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
between the channels and the bulk regions accordingly by

\displaystyle S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\coloneqq\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}}\varepsilon(S_{*}^{\pm}+(k^{\prime},0)),

(2.4)

and the set of lateral boundaries of all channels
N_{\varepsilon}
by

\displaystyle N_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}}\varepsilon(N+(k^{\prime},0)).

(2.5)

Finally, we denote the microscopic domain
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
as the union

\displaystyle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\operatorname{int}\left(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{+}\cup\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}\cup\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{-}\cup S_{*,\varepsilon}^{+}\cup S_{*,\varepsilon}^{-}\right),

(2.6)

see
2.1(b)
for an illustration, and assume it to be Lipschitz regular. For a function
v_{\varepsilon}
defined on
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, we use the superscripts
\pm
and
M
to denote its restriction to the subdomains
\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}
and
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
, respectively, and we also write
v_{\varepsilon}=(v_{\varepsilon}^{+},v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}},v_{\varepsilon}^{-})
.

(a)

The standard cell
Z
contains the reference channel
Z_{*}
(dark green non-cylindric tube) with top and bottom
S_{*}^{\pm}
and lateral boundary
N
.

(b)

The microscopic reference domain
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
consists of two bulk regions
\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}
, being separated by the thin perforated layer
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{\varepsilon}
with microscopic structure
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
. The microstructure of the layer is described by periodic repetition of scaled microcells along the interface
\Sigma
.

Figure 2.1
:
Illustration of the microscopic reference domain
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
in
n=3
dimensions for
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{3}
.

Description of the evolving geometry

For
t\in[0,T]
, the evolving channel domain
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)
and the evolving lateral boundary of the channels
N_{\varepsilon}(t)
are defined using a map

\displaystyle\psi_{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{\varepsilon}}\to\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{\varepsilon}}

(2.7)

with
\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,\cdot)\rvert_{S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}}=\mathrm{id}_{S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}}
, and we write
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)\coloneqq\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})
and
N_{\varepsilon}(t)\coloneqq\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,N_{\varepsilon})
. For further assumptions on
\psi_{\varepsilon}
, we refer to
(T1)
–
(T4)
below.
We introduce the non-cylindrical space–time domains

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{T,\varepsilon,*}^{\mathrm{M}}\coloneqq\left\{(t,x)\,\colon\,t\in(0,T),\,x\in\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\mathcal{N}}_{T,\varepsilon}\coloneqq\left\{(t,x)\,\colon\,t\in(0,T),\,x\in N_{\varepsilon}(t)\right\},

(2.8)

and write

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{T,\varepsilon}\coloneqq\left\{(t,x)\,\colon\,t\in(0,T),\,x\in\operatorname{int}\left(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{+}\cup S_{*,\varepsilon}^{+}\cup\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)\cup S_{*,\varepsilon}^{-}\cup\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{-}\right)\right\}.

(2.9)

The microscopic model

On
{\mathcal{Q}}_{T,\varepsilon}
, we consider a system of reaction–diffusion–advection equations for the unknown function
u_{\varepsilon}=(u_{1\varepsilon},\ldots,u_{m\varepsilon})\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{T,\varepsilon}\to{\mathbbm{R}}^{m}
given by

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\ }c@{\ \, }l}\partial_{t}u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}-\nabla\cdot\left(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}-u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm}\right)&=&f_{j}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})&&\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm},\\
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}-\nabla\cdot\left(\varepsilon D_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}-u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}q_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\right)&=&\frac{1}{\varepsilon}g_{j}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})&&\text{in }{\mathcal{Q}}_{T,\varepsilon,*}^{\mathrm{M}},\end{array}\right.

and subject to the flux boundary conditions

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\ }c@{\ \, }l}-(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}-u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm})\cdot\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times(\partial\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\setminus S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}),\\
-(\varepsilon D_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}-u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}q_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}b_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})\cdot\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}&=&h_{j}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})&&\text{on }{\mathcal{N}}_{T,\varepsilon},\end{array}\right.

on the exterior boundary and the lateral channel walls, respectively, where
b_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}(t,\cdot)=\partial_{t}\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,\psi_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(t,\cdot))
is the velocity of the evolving boundary
N_{\varepsilon}(t)
, and
\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}
denotes the outer unit normal of
\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}
and
\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}=\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}(t)
the outer unit normal of
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)
with respect to
N_{\varepsilon}(t)
. From a physical point of view, the flux term induced by
b_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
describes that when the solute separates from the carrier phase to become part of the channel walls excess solute is pushed away by the movement of the lateral boundaries of the channels.
As initial conditions, we require

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon}(0)=U_{\varepsilon,0}\qquad\text{in }\Omega_{\varepsilon}(0)

(2.10e)

for given functions
U_{\varepsilon,0}\colon\Omega_{\varepsilon}(0)\to{\mathbbm{R}}^{m}
.
We further impose the natural transmission conditions

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\ }c@{\ \, }l}u_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}&=&u_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}&&\text{on }(0,T)\times S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm},\\
\left(-D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}+u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm}\right)\cdot\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}&=&\left(-\varepsilon D_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}+u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}q_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\right)\cdot\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}&&\text{on }(0,T)\times S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm},\end{array}\right.

i.e. the continuity of the solutions and of the total normal fluxes across
S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
.
For the detailed assumptions on the diffusion coefficients
D_{\varepsilon}
and the advective velocity
q_{\varepsilon}
, the reaction rates
f
and
g
, the adsorption/desorption rate
h
, as well as the initial conditions
U_{\varepsilon,0}
, we refer to
(D1)
–
(D4)
below.

Remark 2.1

(Mass conservation)
.

We call
m_{\varepsilon}(t)=\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}u_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}(t,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)}u_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}(t,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
the total mass of
u_{\varepsilon}
, where the prefactor
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
takes into account the shrinking of the domain
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)
to the interface
\Sigma
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
Assuming further that the reaction terms
f
and
g
and the adsorption/desorption term
h
vanish for non-positive concentrations
u_{\varepsilon}
, it follows by standard arguments that
u_{\varepsilon}
, and thus also
m_{\varepsilon}
, is non-negative. Moreover, an application of Reynold’s transport theorem shows that, in the case of completely vanishing nonlinearities
f,\,g,\,h
, sufficiently regular solutions
u_{\varepsilon}
of (
2.10
) are mass-conserving.

We introduce the following concept of weak solutions to (
2.10
). To avoid the usage of generalised time derivatives for functions in Bochner spaces with values in time-dependent Banach spaces, we formally integrate by parts using Reynold’s transport theorem and choose test functions
\varphi\in C^{1}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega})^{m}
in what follows.

Definition 2.2

(Weak solution of (
2.10
))
.

A function
u_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}(t))^{m})
with
u_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}=u_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
on
(0,T)\times S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
is called
weak solution of (
2.10
)
if for all
\varphi\in C^{1}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega})^{m}
with
\varphi(T,\cdot)=0
there holds

\displaystyle\begin{split}&-\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}\partial_{t}\varphi_{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)}u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\partial_{t}\varphi_{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)}D_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad-\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)}u_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}q_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}f_{j}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})\varphi_{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)}g_{j}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{N_{\varepsilon}(t)}h_{j}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}U_{j\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}\,\varphi_{j}(0)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(0)}U_{j\varepsilon,0}^{\mathrm{M}}\,\varphi_{j}(0)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.\end{split}

(2.11)

Assumptions on the transformation

We assume that the transformation
\psi_{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}\to{\mathbbm{R}}^{n}
is such that for all
t\in[0,T]
, the map
\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,\cdot)
is a bijection onto its image
\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}(t)\coloneqq\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}})
and a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism from
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
to
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)\coloneqq\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})
. We further make the following assumptions, where we denote
\psi_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(t,\cdot)\coloneqq\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,\cdot)^{-1}
,
F_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{x}\psi_{\varepsilon}
, and
J_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\det(F_{\varepsilon})
.

(T1)

For all
t\in[0,T]
, we have
\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,\cdot)\rvert_{S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}}=\mathrm{id}_{S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}}
,
\bar{\psi}_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq(\mathrm{id_{\overline{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{+}}}},\psi_{\varepsilon},\mathrm{id_{\overline{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{-}}}})\in C^{1}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega_{\varepsilon}})^{n}
, and there holds

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\lVert\psi_{\varepsilon}-\mathrm{id}_{\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}}\rVert_{\smash{C^{0}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\lVert\partial_{t}\psi_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{C^{0}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\lVert F_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{C^{0}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1.

(2.12)

(T2)

We have
J_{\varepsilon}=\det(\mathrm{D}_{x}\bar{\psi}_{\varepsilon})\in C^{1}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega_{\varepsilon}})
and
J_{\varepsilon}\simeq 1
uniformly on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}
as well as

\displaystyle\lVert\partial_{t}J_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\varepsilon\lVert\nabla J_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1.

(2.13)

Here, the space
{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}
is topologically isomorphic to
H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
, but equipped with an appropriately
\varepsilon
-scaled scalar product, see
Section 3
below.

(T3)

For all
l^{\prime}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}^{n-1}
and
\varepsilon>0
with
|l^{\prime}\varepsilon|\ll h
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\lVert\delta_{l^{\prime},\varepsilon}\partial_{t}\psi_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\widehat{\Omega}^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon,h})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\lVert\delta_{l^{\prime},\varepsilon}F_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{C^{0}([0,T]\times\overline{\widehat{\Omega}^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon,h}})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\varepsilon\lVert\delta_{l^{\prime},\varepsilon}\nabla J_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\widehat{\Omega}^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon,h})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}

\displaystyle\lesssim|l^{\prime}\varepsilon|.

(2.14)

Here,
\widehat{\Omega}^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon,h}
is a suitably chosen subset of
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
such that, for functions
v_{\varepsilon}\colon(0,T)\times\widehat{\Omega}^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon,h}\to{\mathbbm{R}}
, shifts
\delta_{l^{\prime},\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}
of the form
\delta_{l^{\prime},\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\coloneqq v_{\varepsilon}(t,x+(l^{\prime},0)\varepsilon)-v_{\varepsilon}(t,x)
, where
l^{\prime}\in{\mathbbm{Z}}^{n-1}
and
\varepsilon>0
, are well-defined (see
Subsection 3.2
).

(T4)

There exists a function
\psi_{0}\in C^{0}(\overline{\Sigma};C^{1}([0,T]\times\overline{Z_{*}})^{n})
such that, for all
(t,x^{\prime})\in(0,T)\times\Sigma
, the map
\psi_{0}(t,x^{\prime},\cdot_{z})
is a bijection onto its image
\overline{Z_{*}}(t,x^{\prime})\coloneqq\psi_{0}(t,x^{\prime},\overline{Z_{*}})
and a
C^{1}
–diffeomorphism from
Z_{*}
to
Z_{*}(t,x^{\prime})\coloneqq\psi_{0}(t,x^{\prime},Z_{*})
. Moreover, in the strong two-scale sense (see
Definition 4.1
below) in any
L^{p}
-space,
p\in[1,\infty)
, there holds

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\psi_{\varepsilon}-\mathrm{id}_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}})\to\psi_{0}-\mathrm{id}_{Z_{*}},\qquad F_{\varepsilon}\to\mathrm{D}_{z}\psi_{0},\qquad F_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\to\mathrm{D}_{z}\psi_{0}^{-1},\qquad\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}\psi_{\varepsilon}\to\partial_{t}\psi_{0}.

(2.15)

We emphasise that the evolving domain
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)
,
t\in[0,T]
, does not need to be periodic, but only locally periodic, where the estimate on
\psi_{\varepsilon}-\mathrm{id}_{\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}}
in (
2.12
) quantitatively describes the deviation between the time-dependent layer
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)
and the (periodic) reference configuration
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
. For a detailed discussion of the previous assumptions in the context of porous media with evolving microstructure, we refer to
[
GNP21
,
Wie22
,
Wie24
]
. Using the extension property of
\psi_{\varepsilon}
stated in
(T1)
, it is convenient not to distinguish between
\psi_{\varepsilon}
as a map on
\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}
and
\overline{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}
, and similarly for
J_{\varepsilon}
and
J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
.
The assumptions on the shifts given in
(T3)
gain importance when establishing strong two-scale convergence, see
Lemma 3.6
. We emphasise that the estimates (
2.12
) already yield two-scale convergences as stated in
(T4)
but only in the weak sense and only for a subsequence (see
Lemma A.1
); hence, the requirement of strong two-scale convergence is an additional assumption.
The following remark summarises additional two-scale convergence results obtained from the previously stated assumptions.

Remark 2.3

(Two-scale convergences)
.

For the mathematical analysis to follow, the two-scale convergence of
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\psi_{\varepsilon}-\mathrm{id}_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}})
is not needed (but see
Remark 4.2
). However, from a modelling point of view it guarantees that the limit deformation stays close to the identity, which can also be seen using that
\psi_{\varepsilon}\to(\mathrm{id}_{\Sigma},0)
in the strong two-scale sense by
(T4)
.
Moreover, rewriting
F_{\varepsilon}
in terms of the cofactor matrix, we also have
F_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\to F_{0}^{-1}
, and similarly
J_{\varepsilon}\to J_{0}\coloneqq\det(\mathrm{D}_{z}\psi_{0})
, in the strong two-scale sense by
Lemma A.5
. In particular, by the characterisation of two-scale convergence in
Lemma A.4
we have that, for all
t\in(0,T)
,
\psi_{0}(t,\cdot)\rvert_{\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm}}=\mathrm{id}_{\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm}}
and, hence,
J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\equiv 1
on
(0,T)\times\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm}
.

The following remark gives a possible construction of an admissible transformation
\psi_{\varepsilon}
by describing the evolution of the channels locally in each microcell which is of particular interest in applications.

Remark 2.4

(Local construction of
\psi_{\varepsilon}
)
.

We consider a function
\psi_{*}\colon[0,T]\times\overline{\Sigma}\times\overline{Z}\to\overline{Z}
with the following properties:

(i)

\psi_{*}\in C^{1}([0,T];C^{2}(\overline{\Sigma}\times\overline{Z}))
with
\lVert\psi_{*}\rVert_{\smash{C^{1}([0,T];C^{2}(\overline{\Sigma}\times\overline{Z}))}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1
and
\det\mathrm{D}_{z}\psi_{*}\gtrsim 1
.

(ii)

There exists
\delta\in(0,\mathrm{dist}(N,\partial Z))
sufficiently small such that

\displaystyle\psi_{*}(t,x^{\prime},z)=z\qquad\text{for all }(t,x^{\prime},z)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Sigma}\times B_{\delta}(\partial Z).

(2.16)

Then, after extending
\psi_{*}(t,x^{\prime},\cdot)
periodically to
{\mathbbm{R}}^{n-1}\times[-1,1]
with periodicity cell
Y
, for
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}
we define

\displaystyle\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\varepsilon\left(\left\lfloor\tfrac{x^{\prime}}{\varepsilon}\right\rfloor,0\right)+\varepsilon\psi_{*}\left(t,\varepsilon\left\lfloor\tfrac{x^{\prime}}{\varepsilon}\right\rfloor,\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}-\left(\left\lfloor\tfrac{x^{\prime}}{\varepsilon}\right\rfloor,0\right)\right)=\varepsilon\left(\left\lfloor\tfrac{x^{\prime}}{\varepsilon}\right\rfloor,0\right)+\varepsilon\psi_{*}\left(t,\varepsilon\left\lfloor\tfrac{x^{\prime}}{\varepsilon}\right\rfloor,\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}\right).

(2.17)

The incorporation of the variable
x^{\prime}\in\overline{\Sigma}
allows the description of a non-periodic microstructure of the time-dependent layer
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t)
.
Then, a direct calculation shows that
\psi_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}([0,T];C^{2}(\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}))
as well as the validity of the estimates in
(T1)
and
(T2)
.
Similarly, the estimates for the shifts in
(T3)
are obtained using the mean-value theorem.
Defining the map
\psi_{0}\colon[0,T]\times\overline{\Sigma}\times\overline{Z_{*}}\to{\mathbbm{R}}^{n}
by

\displaystyle\psi_{0}(t,x^{\prime},z)=(x^{\prime},0)+\psi_{*}(t,x^{\prime},z),

(2.18)

the two-scale convergences in
(T4)
are a direct consequence of the oscillation lemma on thin domains, see
[
NJ07
, Lemma 4.3]
, and the uniform convergence
\varepsilon\left\lfloor\tfrac{x^{\prime}}{\varepsilon}\right\rfloor\to x^{\prime}
.

Structural assumptions on the data

In order to obtain the existence of (weak) microsolutions and suitable a priori estimates, we make the following assumptions on the data
D_{\varepsilon}
,
q_{\varepsilon}
, and
U_{\varepsilon,0}
as well as on the terms
f
,
g
and
h
:

(D1)

The diffusion coefficients
D_{j\varepsilon}=(D_{j}^{+},D_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}},D_{j}^{-})\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Q}}_{T,\varepsilon})^{n\times n}
satisfy
\lVert D_{j\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Q}}_{T,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1
and are strongly uniformly elliptic uniformly in
\varepsilon
, i.e. for any
\xi\in{\mathbbm{R}}^{n}
we have
\xi^{T}D_{j\varepsilon}\xi\gtrsim|\xi|^{2}
uniformly in
(t,x)\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{T,\varepsilon}
.

(D2)

The advection velocities
q_{j\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Q}}_{T,\varepsilon})^{n}
satisfy
\lVert q_{j\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Q}}_{T,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1
.

(D3)

The initial conditions
U_{\varepsilon,0}=(U_{0}^{+},U_{\varepsilon,0}^{\mathrm{M}},U_{0}^{-})\colon\Omega_{\varepsilon}(0)\to{\mathbbm{R}}^{m}
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\lVert U_{0}^{\pm}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}(0))}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\lVert U_{\varepsilon,0}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(0))}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1.

(2.19)

(D4)

The source/sink terms
f_{j},\,g_{j},\,h_{j}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times{\mathbbm{R}}^{m})
are globally Lipschitz continuous in
z\in{\mathbbm{R}}^{m}
uniformly with respect to
t\in(0,T)
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle|f_{j}(t,z)|+|g_{j}(t,z)|+|h_{j}(t,z)|\lesssim 1+|z|

(2.20)

uniformly in
t\in(0,T)
.

Remark 2.5

(Assumptions on the reaction rates)
.

To focus on the main aspects of this paper and for the sake of readability, we assume that the source/sink terms
f
,
g
and
h
are independent of the spatial variable. However, with only slight modifications of the proofs, the results of this work still can be obtained in this more general case if it is assumed that
f
,
g
and
h
are uniformly Lipschitz continuous with respect to the concentration variable. Then, after applying the transformation
\psi_{\varepsilon}
, one obtains an additional
\varepsilon
-dependency of the nonlinearities
g
and
h
, and it is even possible to consider
\varepsilon
-dependent nonlinearities right from the beginning if their two-scale convergence still can be guaranteed (see e.g.
[
GN21
, Lemma 3.6]
). It is further possible to relax the assumption on the Lipschitz continuity of the reaction rates by only requiring the Lipschitz continuity of the maps
z\mapsto[f_{j}(z)z]_{+}
,
z\mapsto[g_{j}(z)z]_{+}
, and
z\mapsto[h_{j}(z)z]_{-}
, where
[\cdot]_{\pm}
denotes the positive/negative part, and absorbing the corresponding negative/positive part in the operator of the boundary value problem in a coercivity-preserving way.

Estimates on the shifts of the data

The proof of strong two-scale convergence of the microsolutions is based on the following control of the shifts
D_{\varepsilon}
,
q_{\varepsilon}
, and
U_{\varepsilon,0}
. As this property is needed only for the transformed problem on the reference domain
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, we formulate it for the functions

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}=D_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\circ_{x}\psi_{\varepsilon},\qquad\bar{q}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}=q_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\circ_{x}\psi_{\varepsilon},\qquad\tilde{U}_{\varepsilon,0}=U_{\varepsilon,0}\circ\psi_{\varepsilon}(0,\cdot).

(2.21)

(S1)

The diffusion coefficients
D_{\varepsilon}
satisfy
\lVert\delta_{l^{\prime},\varepsilon}\bar{D}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\widehat{\Omega}^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon,h})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim|l^{\prime}\varepsilon|
.

(S2)

The advection velocities
q_{\varepsilon}
satisfy
\lVert\delta_{l^{\prime},\varepsilon}\bar{q}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\widehat{\Omega}^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon,h})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim|l^{\prime}\varepsilon|
.

(S3)

The initial conditions
U_{\varepsilon,0}
satisfy
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\lVert\delta_{l^{\prime},\varepsilon}\tilde{U}_{j\varepsilon,0}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\widehat{\Omega}^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon,h})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\to 0
as
\varepsilon l^{\prime}\to 0
.

Asymptotic behaviour of the data

The limit behaviour of the functions
D_{\varepsilon}
,
q_{\varepsilon}
, and
U_{\varepsilon,0}
is assumed to be as follows:

(A1)

There exists a strongly uniformly elliptic function
\bar{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})^{n\times n}
such that
\bar{D}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\to\bar{D}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}
in the strong two-scale sense.

(A2)

There exists a function
\bar{q}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})^{n}
such that
\bar{q}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\to\bar{q}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}
in the strong two-scale sense.

(A3)

There exists a function
\tilde{U}_{0,0}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{2}(\Sigma\times Z_{*})^{n}
such that
J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}(0)\tilde{U}_{j\varepsilon,0}^{\mathrm{M}}\to J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}(0)\tilde{U}_{j0,0}^{\mathrm{M}}
in the strong two-scale sense.

Here, the two-scale convergence is assumed to hold with respect to test functions in
L^{2}
. In
(A3)
, we do not assume the (strong) two-scale convergence of the sequence
(\tilde{U}_{j\varepsilon,0}^{\mathrm{M}})_{\varepsilon}
as the natural initial conditions for the microscopic problem are given in terms of the product
J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}(0)\tilde{U}_{j\varepsilon,0}^{\mathrm{M}}
, also see
Definition 3.1
and
Corollary 4.6

(iii)
below.

3
Existence of microscopic solutions and a priori estimates

We introduce a further concept of weak solutions to (
2.10
) by transforming the weak formulation (
2.11
) to the static reference domain
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
. This turns out to suitable for the application of standard tools from the theory of periodic homogenisation in thin layers, which enables us to pass to the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
in
Section 4
. The main ingredients for the limit passage are the a priori estimates and estimates for the shifts of the microsolutions derived in this section.

Given a function
u_{\varepsilon}
on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{T,\varepsilon,*}^{\mathrm{M}}
, we denote by
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
its evaluation in the coordinates on
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
, that is for
(t,\tilde{x})\in(0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
we have
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(t,\tilde{x})=u_{\varepsilon}(t,\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,\tilde{x}))
. By the chain rule, we have

\displaystyle\nabla u_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=F_{\varepsilon}^{-\top}\nabla_{\tilde{x}}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\rvert_{(t,\psi_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(t,x))},

(3.1)

whereas the surface measure transforms according to

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}\,\raisebox{-0.5468pt}{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\lnot$}}}\,{N_{\varepsilon}(t)}=J_{\varepsilon}\lVert F_{\varepsilon}^{-\top}\tilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}\,\raisebox{-0.5468pt}{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\lnot$}}}\,{N_{\varepsilon}},

(3.2)

see
[
MH94
, p. 117]
and the explicit calculation in three dimensions given in
[
Cia88
, p. 39]
, where
\tilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
is the outer normal of
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
with respect to
N_{\varepsilon}
. To keep notation simpler, we always write
\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}=\tilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
in what follows.

Following the approach of
[
GN21
]
, we further introduce Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}
with inner products adapted to the scaling of the equation in the channel domain
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
. We begin with

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{+})\times L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})\times L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{-}),

(3.3)

equipped with the inner product

\displaystyle\left(v_{\varepsilon},w_{\varepsilon}\right)_{{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}}\coloneqq\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\left(v_{\varepsilon}^{\pm},w_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}},w_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})},

(3.4)

and define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\left\{v_{\varepsilon}=(v_{\varepsilon}^{+},v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}},v_{\varepsilon}^{-})\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{+})\times H^{1}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})\times H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{-})\,\colon\,v_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}=v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\text{ on }S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\right\}

(3.5)

with inner product

\displaystyle\left(v_{\varepsilon},w_{\varepsilon}\right)_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}}\coloneqq\left(v_{\varepsilon},w_{\varepsilon}\right)_{{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}}+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\left(\nabla v_{\varepsilon}^{\pm},\nabla w_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}+\varepsilon\left(\nabla v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}},\nabla w_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}.

(3.6)

To shorten notation, we write
{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
for the space of restrictions to
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
of functions belonging to
{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}
.
Clearly, we have
{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}=L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}=H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
in a topological sense by identifying the latter spaces with the product spaces on the different subdomains, and consequently
{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\xhookrightarrow{}{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
is a Gelfand triple. Assuming that a function
v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon})
with
\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})
is more regular in the sense that
\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\in L^{2}(0,T;(H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}))^{\prime})
and
\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{2}(0,T;(H^{1}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}))^{\prime})
, from the previous Gelfand triple we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon},\varphi_{\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\left\langle\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}^{\pm},\varphi_{\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{(H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}))^{\prime},\,H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\langle\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}},\varphi_{\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{(H^{1}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}))^{\prime},\,H^{1}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})},

(3.7)

which shows how the generalised time derivative of
v_{\varepsilon}
is related to the scaling of the weak equation in the thin layer
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
.
To simplify the writing and using the notation from (
2.21
), we introduce the transformed data

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}=F_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\bar{D}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}F_{\varepsilon}^{-\top},\qquad\tilde{q}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}=F_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\bar{q}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}},\qquad\tilde{b}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}=-\partial_{t}\psi_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\circ_{x}\psi_{\varepsilon}=F_{\varepsilon}^{-1}b_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\circ_{x}\psi_{\varepsilon}=F_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\partial_{t}\psi_{\varepsilon}

(3.8)

and recall that
\tilde{U}_{\varepsilon,0}=U_{\varepsilon,0}\circ\psi_{\varepsilon}(0,\cdot)
.
We choose test functions
\Phi_{\varepsilon}=\varphi_{\varepsilon}\circ_{x}\psi_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
for some
\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega_{\varepsilon}})^{m}
in (
2.11
), apply the inverse transformation
\psi_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
and integrate by parts in time for sufficiently regular
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
by taking into account (
3.7
). Then, the weak formulation (
2.11
) yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\langle\partial_{t}(J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}),\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}}+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\varepsilon\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{D}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\\
&\quad-\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{q}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{b}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}f_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}g_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-\int_{N_{\varepsilon}}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\lVert F_{\varepsilon}^{-\top}\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert h_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(x)\end{split}

(3.9)

for all
\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})^{m}
and almost every
t\in(0,T)
. Here, the appearance of the term involving
\tilde{b}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
is a consequence of the chain rule applied to
\varphi
. To use a unified notation, we consider weak solutions
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
as functions on
(0,T)\times\Omega_{\varepsilon}
taking into account the extension property of
\psi_{\varepsilon}
stated in
(T1)
.

Definition 3.1

(Weak solution)
.

A function
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{m})
with
\partial_{t}(J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon})\in L^{2}(0,T;({\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{m})^{\prime})
is called
weak solution of the reference problem
if for all
\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{m}
and almost every
t\in(0,T)
there holds (
3.9
)
and the initial condition
(J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon})(0)=J_{\varepsilon}(0)\tilde{U}_{\varepsilon,0}
is satisfied a.e. in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
.

By standard embedding theorems for Sobolev–Bochner spaces, we have
J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\in C^{0}([0,T];{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}^{m})
; hence, the initial condition is well-posed. Due to the regularity of
\partial_{t}(J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon})
and
J_{\varepsilon}
, we further have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}=J_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\partial_{t}(J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon})-J_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}J_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;({\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{m})^{\prime}),

(3.10)

and, therefore,
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\in C^{0}([0,T];{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}^{m})
. Consequently, the initial conditions
(J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon})(0)=J_{\varepsilon}(0)\tilde{U}_{\varepsilon,0}
and
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(0)=\tilde{U}_{\varepsilon,0}
are equivalent.

Remark 3.2

(Strong formulation of (
3.9
))
.

For a sufficiently regular weak solution
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
the strong problem associated with (
3.9
) is given by

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\ }c@{\ \, }l}\partial_{t}u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}-\nabla\cdot\left(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}-u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm}\right)&=&f_{j}(u_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})&&\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm},\\
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}(J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})-\nabla\cdot\left(\varepsilon J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{D}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}-J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{q}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{b}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\right)&=&\frac{1}{\varepsilon}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}g_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})&&\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon},\end{array}\right.

equipped with the boundary conditions

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\ }c@{\ \, }l}-(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}-u_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm})\cdot\nu^{\pm}&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times(\partial\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\setminus S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}),\\
-(\varepsilon\tilde{D}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}-\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{q}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{b}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})\cdot\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}&=&\lVert F_{\varepsilon}^{-\top}\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert h_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})&&\text{on }(0,T)\times N_{\varepsilon},\end{array}\right.

the initial condition
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(0)=\tilde{U}_{\varepsilon,0}
and the transmission conditions

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\ }c@{\ \, }l}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}&=&\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}&&\text{on }(0,T)\times S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm},\\
\left(-D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}+\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm}\right)\cdot\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}&=&\left(-\varepsilon\tilde{D}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}+\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{q}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\right)\cdot\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}&&\text{on }(0,T)\times S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}.\end{array}\right.

We emphasise that the boundary conditions and the transmission conditions are incorporated in the weak formulation (
3.9
) and the definition of the test space
{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}
implicitly.

3.1
A priori estimates

We first recall the following trace inequality for functions defined on
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 3.3

(Trace estimate)
.

For every
\theta\in(0,1]
and
v_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})
, there holds

\displaystyle\int_{N_{\varepsilon}}|v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(x)\lesssim\theta\varepsilon\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}|\nabla v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{\theta\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}|v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

(3.12)

Proof.

Decomposing
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
into (shifted and scaled) microcells
\varepsilon(Z_{*}+(k^{\prime},0))
, where
k^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\varepsilon}
, transforming the domain of integration to
Z_{*}
, and using the weighted trace inequality for Lipschitz domains as given e.g. in
[
Gri85
, Theorem 1.5.1.10]
on
\partial Z_{*}
, the result is directly obtained.
∎

For the weak solutions
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
of (
3.9
), we have the following a priori estimates.

Lemma 3.4

(A priori estimates)
.

The weak solutions
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
of (
3.9
) satisfy the a priori estimates

\displaystyle\lVert\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{\infty}(0,T;{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}^{m})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\lVert\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{m})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,

(3.13a)

\displaystyle\lVert\partial_{t}(J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon})\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T;({\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{m})^{\prime})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\lVert J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{m})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1,

(3.13b)

where the constants only depend on
T
and the constants from
(T1)
,
(D1)
–
(D4)
and
Lemma 3.3
.

Proof.

Similar estimates were proved by analogous arguments in
[
GNP21
, Lemma 1]
for periodically perforated bulk domains, and in
[
GN21
, Lemma 2.3]
for non-evolving channels in the thin layer
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{\varepsilon}
, that is for
J_{\varepsilon}\equiv 1
. As
{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}\xhookrightarrow{}{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}\xhookrightarrow{}{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
is a Gelfand triple, due to the regularity in time of
J_{\varepsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\lVert\sqrt{J_{\varepsilon}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\partial_{t}(\sqrt{J_{\varepsilon}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}),\sqrt{J_{\varepsilon}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}}

(3.14)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\partial_{t}(J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}),\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}}-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle(\partial_{t}J_{\varepsilon})\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon},\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}}

(3.15)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\partial_{t}(J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}),\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}}-\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\left(\partial_{t}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}},(\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})^{2}\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}.

(3.16)

Rewriting
F_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
in terms of the cofactor matrix by Cramer’s rule, we also have
\lVert F_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\rVert_{\smash{C^{0}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1
. By
(D1)
and
(T1)
the diffusion coefficients
\tilde{D}_{j\varepsilon}
are strongly uniformly elliptic uniformly in
\varepsilon
.
Testing the weak formulation (
3.9
) with the function
\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}
itself, using
J_{\varepsilon}\gtrsim 1
, we thus obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\lVert\sqrt{J_{\varepsilon}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2}+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2}+\varepsilon\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2}

(3.17)

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm}\cdot\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{q}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{b}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(3.18)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}\partial_{t}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}(\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}f_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}g_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(3.19)

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{N_{\varepsilon}}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\lVert F_{\varepsilon}^{-\top}\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert h_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(x)\eqqcolon\sum_{i=1}^{7}I_{\varepsilon}^{(i)}.

(3.20)

We estimate the terms
I_{\varepsilon}^{(i)}
separately and obtain for any
\theta\in(0,1]
by Young’s inequality

\displaystyle|I_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\lVert q_{j}^{\pm}\rVert_{\smash{L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lVert\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\theta}\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\lVert\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2}+\theta\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2},

(3.21)

and similarly

\displaystyle|I_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\theta\varepsilon}\lVert\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2}+\theta\varepsilon\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2},

(3.22)

where we have used
\tilde{q}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})
with
\lVert\tilde{q}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1
which follows from
(T1)
and
(D2)
.
As we have no uniform bound
\lVert\partial_{t}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{C^{0}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1
at hand, we rewrite
\partial_{t}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}=\nabla\cdot(J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{b}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})
in
I_{\varepsilon}^{(4)}
, which is a direct consequence of Jacobi’s formula for the determinant and the divergence-freeness of the cofactor matrix by Piola’s identity (see
[
MH94
, p. 117]
). Together with
\tilde{b}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rvert_{S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}}\equiv 0
we then obtain

\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}^{(3)}+I_{\varepsilon}^{(4)}=-\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{N_{\varepsilon}}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{b}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}(\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(x).

(3.23)

As
\lVert\tilde{b}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{C^{0}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim\varepsilon
by
(T1)
, with the trace estimate in
Lemma 3.3
we conclude

\displaystyle|I_{\varepsilon}^{(3)}+I_{\varepsilon}^{(4)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\theta\varepsilon}\lVert\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2}+\theta\varepsilon\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2}.

(3.24)

Finally, we estimate the integrals involving the nonlinearities
f
,
g
, and
h
. Using
(T1)
, the growth condition (
2.20
) on
h_{j}
,
|N_{\varepsilon}|\simeq 1
and the trace estimate in
Lemma 3.3
, we calculate

\displaystyle|I_{\varepsilon}^{(7)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{N_{\varepsilon}}(1+|\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}|)|\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(x)\lesssim|N_{\varepsilon}|+\int_{N_{\varepsilon}}|\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(x)

(3.25)

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\frac{1}{\theta\varepsilon}\lVert\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2}+\theta\varepsilon\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2}.

(3.26)

Similarly, using the growth conditions (
2.20
) for
f_{j}
and
g_{j}
as well as
|\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}|\simeq 1
and
|\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}|\simeq\varepsilon
, we obtain

\displaystyle|I_{\varepsilon}^{(5)}|\lesssim 1+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\lVert\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2},\qquad|I_{\varepsilon}^{(6)}|\lesssim 1+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\lVert\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2}.

(3.27)

Collecting the previous estimates, choosing
\theta>0
sufficiently small to absorb the gradient terms and summing over
j
, we finally obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\lVert\sqrt{J_{\varepsilon}}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}^{m}}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2}+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2}+\varepsilon\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2}\lesssim 1+\lVert\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}^{m}}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}^{2}.

(3.28)

Integrating with respect to time, using
J_{\varepsilon}\simeq 1
and
\tilde{U}_{\varepsilon,0}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}^{m}
with
\lVert\tilde{U}_{\varepsilon,0}\rVert_{\smash{{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}^{m}}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1
by
(D3)
, Gronwall’s inequality implies
\lVert J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}^{m}}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\simeq\lVert\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}^{m}}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1
. As the latter estimate is independent of
t\in(0,T)
, we deduce (
3.13a
) from (
3.28
). Using (
2.13
), we further obtain the second estimate in (
3.13b
) as

\displaystyle\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lVert\nabla(J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}

(3.29)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lVert J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lVert\nabla J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim\lVert\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{m}}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}.

(3.30)

By a similar reasoning as before but using an arbitrary test function
\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{m}
instead of
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
, we obtain

\displaystyle|\left\langle\partial_{t}(J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}),\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}}|

(3.31)

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\left|\left(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm},\nabla\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\right|+\varepsilon\left|\left(J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{D}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}},\nabla\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\right|+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\left|\left(\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm},\nabla\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\right|

(3.32)

\displaystyle\quad+\left|\left(J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{q}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}},\nabla\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\right|+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left|\left(J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}b_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}},\nabla\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\right|+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\left|\left(f_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}),\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\right|

(3.33)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left|\left(J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}g_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}),\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\right|+\left|\left(J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\lVert F_{\varepsilon}^{-\top}\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert h_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}),\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\right)_{L^{2}(N_{\varepsilon})}\right|

(3.34)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\left(\lVert\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\right)\lVert\nabla\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\varepsilon\lVert\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lVert\nabla\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}

(3.35)

\displaystyle\quad+\lVert\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lVert\nabla\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\left(1+\lVert\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\right)\lVert\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}

(3.36)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\lVert\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\right)\lVert\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\lVert\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{({\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})^{m}}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lVert\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}.

(3.37)

Summing over
j
, we deduce

\displaystyle\lVert\partial_{t}(J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon})\rVert_{\smash{({\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{m})^{\prime}}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1+\lVert\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{m}}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}},

(3.38)

and thereby (
3.13b
) by the estimates for
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
in (
3.13a
) after integrating with respect to time.
∎

As discussed in
[
GNP21
, Remark 2]
for the case of bulk domains, under the given assumptions and by (
3.10
) we have
\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})
but cannot deduce a uniform bound for
\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
in
L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})
from this. However, further assuming
\lVert\partial_{t}J_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1
we could improve this to obtain such a uniform bound.

Using the Galerkin method, estimates similar to that established in
Lemma 3.4
and a fixed-point argument, we obtain the existence of weak solutions.

Lemma 3.5

(Existence of a weak solution)
.

There exists a unique weak solution
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
of (
3.11
).

Proof.

To prove existence of a weak solution
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
of (
3.11
), we rewrite (
3.9
) as an equivalent equation for
w_{\varepsilon}=J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
using the spatial regularity of
J_{\varepsilon}>0
. For all
\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{m}
and almost every
t\in(0,T)
it reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\langle\partial_{t}w_{j\varepsilon},\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}}\\
