. Consequently, this notion of two-scale convergence for functions naturally induces the notion of two-scale convergence given in
Definition 4.1
.

On the other hand, it can be shown that a sequence of functions
v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{p}(0,T;\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}(t))
converges to
v_{0}\in L^{p}((0,T)\times\Sigma;Z_{*}(t,x^{\prime}))
in the two-scale sense of (
4.5
) if and only if the sequence of transformed functions
\tilde{v}_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq v_{\varepsilon}\circ_{x}\psi_{\varepsilon}
converges to
\tilde{v}_{0}\coloneqq v_{0}\circ_{z}\psi_{0}
in the two-scale sense as given by
Definition 4.1
. This is achieved by adapting the arguments given in
[
Wie22
, Section 3]
for the case of (porous) bulk domains as the proofs are mainly based on unfolding techniques, and the unfolding operator introduced in
Definition A.2
enjoys similar properties. At this point, the two-scale convergence
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\psi_{\varepsilon}-\mathrm{id}_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}})\to\psi_{0}-\mathrm{id}_{Z_{*}}
is of crucial importance which is our reason to include it in the assumptions on the transformation
\psi_{\varepsilon}
.

The main compactness results we use in what follows are given in
Theorem 4.3
below, which is an extension of
Lemma A.1
(weak two-scale compactness) to functions defined on the whole microscopic domain
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
and
Theorem A.13
(strong two-scale compactness). We write
H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*})=H^{1}_{S_{*}^{+}\cup S_{*}^{-},0}(Z_{*})
for the sake of clearer notation and first introduce the space
{\mathcal{H}}
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}\coloneqq\left\{v=(v^{+},v^{\mathrm{M}},v^{-})\in H^{1}(\Omega^{+})\times L^{2}(\Sigma;H^{1}(Z_{*}))\times H^{1}(\Omega^{-})\,\colon\,v^{\mathrm{M}}=v^{\pm}(\cdot_{x^{\prime}},0)\text{ on }\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm}\right\},

(4.6)

equipped with the inner product

\displaystyle\left(v,w\right)_{{\mathcal{H}}}\coloneqq\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\left(v^{\pm},w^{\pm}\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\pm})}+\left(v^{\mathrm{M}},w^{\mathrm{M}}\right)_{L^{2}(\Sigma\times Z_{*})}+\left(\nabla_{z}v^{\mathrm{M}},\nabla_{z}w^{\mathrm{M}}\right)_{L^{2}(\Sigma\times Z_{*})}.

(4.7)

We note that the transmission condition on
\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm}
implies that
v^{\mathrm{M}}\rvert_{S_{*}^{\pm}}(\cdot_{t},\cdot_{x^{\prime}},z)
is independent of
z\in S_{*}^{\pm}
.
Moreover, we have the Gelfand triple
{\mathcal{H}}\xhookrightarrow{}{\mathcal{L}}\xhookrightarrow{}{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
, where
{\mathcal{L}}\coloneqq L^{2}(\Omega^{+})\times L^{2}(\Sigma\times Z_{*})\times L^{2}(\Omega^{-})
is eqipped with the usual scalar product.
In
Theorem 4.3
below, it turns out that the space
{\mathcal{H}}
is the appropriate choice to capture properties of the limit of the sequence of microsolutions
(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
.
We note that for functions
v\in{\mathcal{H}}
we have
v^{\mathrm{M}}=v^{\pm}(\cdot_{x^{\prime}},0)
on
\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm}
, which is the limit analogue of the transmission conditions
v_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}=v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
on
S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
for functions
v_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}
.
As for functions
\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}}
the evaluation
\varphi(x,\tfrac{x^{\prime}}{\varepsilon})
is not well-defined for
x\in\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}\cup N_{\varepsilon}
, we further consider the subspace of smooth functions

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{\infty}\coloneqq\left[C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega^{+}})\times C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\Sigma;C^{\infty}(\overline{Z_{*}}))\times C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega^{-}})\right]\cap{\mathcal{H}}.

(4.8)

Then, the space
{\mathcal{H}}^{\infty}
is dense in
{\mathcal{H}}
with respect to the norm induced by (
4.7
), see
[
GN21
, Proposition 4.3]
.

Theorem 4.3

(Compactness)
.

Let
v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon})
with
\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})
be functions with

\displaystyle\lVert\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\lVert v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1.

(4.9)

Then, there exists
v_{0}=(v_{0}^{+},v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}},v_{0}^{-})\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}})
with
\partial_{t}v_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})
such that the following properties hold true.

(i)

Convergence in the bulk.
The limits in the bulks satisfy

\displaystyle v_{0}^{\pm}\in L^{2}((0,T);H^{1}(\Omega^{\pm}))\quad\text{with}\quad\partial_{t}v_{0}^{\pm}\in L^{2}(0,T;(H^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega^{\pm}))^{\prime}),

(4.10)

and, for a subsequence, we have

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rclcl}\chi_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}v_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}&\to&v_{0}^{\pm}&&\text{strongly in }L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\pm}),\\
\chi_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}\nabla v_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}&\to&\nabla v_{0}^{\pm}&&\text{weakly in }L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\pm}),\\
v_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\cdot_{t},\cdot_{x^{\prime}},\pm\varepsilon)&\to&v_{0}^{\pm}(\cdot_{t},\cdot_{x^{\prime}},0)&&\text{strongly in }L^{2}((0,T)\times\Sigma).\end{array}

(ii)

Convergence in the layer.

The limit in the layer satisfies

\displaystyle v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Sigma;H^{1}(Z_{*})))\quad\text{with}\quad\partial_{t}v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Sigma;(H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*}))^{\prime}))

(4.14)

and, for a subsequence, we have

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rclcl}v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}&\to&v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}&&\text{in the two-scale sense},\\
\varepsilon\nabla v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}&\to&\nabla_{z}v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}&&\text{in the two-scale sense}.\end{array}

(iii)

Convergence of the time derivative.
For any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}([0,T);{\mathcal{H}}^{\infty})
, writing

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\coloneqq\left\{\begin{array}[]{rclcl}\varphi^{\pm}(t,x^{\prime},x_{n}\mp\varepsilon)&&\text{for }x\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm},\\
\varphi^{\mathrm{M}}\left(t,x^{\prime},\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)&&\text{for }x\in\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon},\\
\end{array}\right.

we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{T}\left\langle\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon},\varphi_{\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\left\langle\partial_{t}v_{0},\varphi\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t.

(4.19a)

In particular,
v_{0}\in C^{0}([0,T];{\mathcal{L}})
and

\displaystyle\left(v_{\varepsilon}(0),\varphi_{\varepsilon}(0)\right)_{{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to}\left(v_{0}(0),\varphi(0)\right)_{{\mathcal{L}}}.

(4.19b)

Proof.

We split the proof in several steps and first show
(i)
and
(ii)
. From this, we conclude the coupling condition between
v_{0}^{\pm}
and
v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
incorporated in the definition of the space
{\mathcal{H}}
and, thus,
v_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}})
. Finally, the proof of
(iii)
is based on the convergence properties obtained in
(i)
and
(ii)
.

To show
(i)
, the arguments given in
[
NJ07
, Section 5.1]
can be followed in general; however, there it is assumed that
\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})
. In our case, by (
3.141
), we only have
\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\in L^{2}(0,T;({\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm})^{\prime})
, but the used change of variables and application of the Aubin–Lions lemma can easily be adapted to this setting (also see
[
GN21
, Proposition 4.1]
).

Next,
(ii)
is a direct consequence of (
3.141
) and the a priori estimates for
v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
obtained from (
4.9
) together with the compactness result in
Lemma A.1

(iii)
, where the regularity of
\partial_{t}v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
is due to
Lemma A.1

(iv)
.

To show
v_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}})
, it remains to establish the coupling condition
v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}(t,x^{\prime},z)=v_{0}^{\pm}(t,x^{\prime},0)
for almost every
(t,x^{\prime},z)\in(0,T)\times\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm}
.
We argue similarly as in
[
GN21
, Theorem 4.2]
(also see
[
NJ07
, Section 5.3]
). By the strong convergence of the traces from
(i)
, we also have
v_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\cdot_{t},\cdot_{x^{\prime}},\pm\varepsilon)\to v_{0}^{\pm}(\cdot_{t},\cdot_{x^{\prime}},0)
in the two-scale sense with respect to test functions in
L^{2}((0,T)\times\Sigma;C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{per}}(\overline{\Sigma_{*}}))
, where
\Sigma_{*}\coloneqq\Sigma\cap Z_{*}
, see
[
All92
, p. 1487]
.
Then, for all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Sigma;C^{\infty}_{\#}(\overline{Z_{*}}))
with compact support with respect to
z\in Z_{*}\cup S_{*}^{+}\cup S_{+}^{-}
, by
(ii)
, using the transmission condition
v_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}=v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
on
(0,T)\times S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
and the previous observation, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}\nabla_{z}v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}(t,x^{\prime},z)\varphi(t,x^{\prime},z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(4.20)

\displaystyle\quad=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}\varepsilon\nabla v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}(t,x)\varphi\left(t,x^{\prime},\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(4.21)

\displaystyle\quad=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left[-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}\left(\varepsilon v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}(t,x)\nabla_{x^{\prime}}\varphi\left(t,x^{\prime},\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)+v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}(t,x)\nabla_{z}\varphi\left(t,x^{\prime},\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\right.

(4.22)

\displaystyle\qquad\phantom{\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\left.\int_{0}^{T}\int_{S_{*,\varepsilon}^{\pm}}\varepsilon v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}(t,x)\varphi\left(t,x^{\prime},\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\right]

(4.23)

\displaystyle\quad=-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}(t,x^{\prime},z)\nabla_{z}\varphi(t,x^{\prime},z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(4.24)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{S_{*}^{\pm}}v_{0}^{\pm}(t,x^{\prime},0)\varphi(t,x^{\prime},z)n_{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t,

(4.25)

where
n_{\pm}=(0^{\prime},\pm 1)\in{\mathbbm{R}}^{n}
. By the divergence theorem together with the choice of the support of
\varphi
, we thus have

\displaystyle\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{S_{*}^{\pm}}\left(v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}(t,x^{\prime},z)-v_{0}^{\pm}(t,x^{\prime},0)\right)\varphi(t,x^{\prime},z)n_{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=0,

(4.26)

which after applying the fundamental lemma of the calculus of variations yields the desired result.

Finally, to obtain
(iii)
, we argue similarly as in
[
GN25
, Lemma B.5]
(also see
[
GNP21
, Proposition 4]
for the case of bulk regions) but have to consider functions defined on the whole domain
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
instead.
Denoting by
\delta_{t}^{h}
difference quotients in time, that is

\displaystyle\delta_{t}^{h}v_{0}^{\pm}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{v_{0}^{\pm}(\cdot_{t}+h,\cdot_{x})-v_{0}^{\pm}}{h}\qquad\text{in }(0,T-h)\times\Omega^{\pm},

(4.27)

\displaystyle\delta_{t}^{h}v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}(\cdot_{t}+h,\cdot_{x^{\prime}},\cdot_{z})-v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}}{h}\qquad\text{in }(0,T-h)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*},

(4.28)

by the reflexivity of
L^{2}(0,T;({\mathcal{H}}^{m})^{\prime})
it suffices to bound
\lVert\delta_{t}^{h}v_{0}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T-h;{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}
uniformly in
h
to conclude
\partial_{t}v_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})
(see
[
GP05
, Proposition 2.2.26]
, or
[
Eva10
, Chapter 5.8, Theorem 3]
for the case of real-valued functions).
For any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T);{\mathcal{H}}^{\infty})
, by the convergence properties of
(v_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
obtained in
(i)
and
(ii)
, we deduce

\displaystyle\left\langle\delta_{t}^{h}v_{0},\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(0,T-h;{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}),\,L^{2}(0,T-h;{\mathcal{H}})}

(4.29)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\pm}\int_{0}^{T-h}\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}\delta_{t}^{h}v_{0}^{\pm}\varphi^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T-h}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}\delta_{t}^{h}v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\varphi^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(4.30)

\displaystyle\quad=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{T-h}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}\delta_{t}^{h}v_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{T-h}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}\delta_{t}^{h}v_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(4.31)

\displaystyle\quad=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{T-h}\left\langle\delta_{t}^{h}v_{\varepsilon},\varphi_{\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\leq\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lVert\delta_{t}^{h}v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T-h;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lVert\varphi_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T-h;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}.

(4.32)

As
\lVert\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T;({\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{m})^{\prime})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1
, we also have
\lVert\delta_{t}^{h}v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T-h;({\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{m})^{\prime})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1
uniformly in
h
and it suffices to bound
\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lVert\varphi_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T-h;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}
. By an explicit calculation using the oscillation lemma for thin domains (see
[
NJ07
, Lemma 4.3]
) we have
\lVert\varphi_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T-h;{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\to\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T-h;{\mathcal{H}})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, and thus obtain

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\delta_{t}^{h}v_{0},\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}),\,L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}})}\right|\lesssim\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T-h;{\mathcal{H}})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}},

(4.33)

which by the density of
{\mathcal{H}}^{\infty}
in the space
{\mathcal{H}}
extends to functions
\varphi\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}})
and therefore yields
\partial_{t}v_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})
. The convergence (
4.19a
) for test functions
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T);{\mathcal{H}}^{\infty})
then follows similarly as in
[
GNP21
, Proposition 4]
and
[
GN25
, Lemma B.5]
by the definition of the generalised time derivative.
Using a cut-off function in time near
0
, the previous convergence also holds true for functions
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}([0,T);{\mathcal{H}}^{\infty})
.
We thus further have
v_{0}\in C^{0}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega^{+})\times L^{2}(\Sigma\times Z_{*})\times L^{2}(\Omega^{-}))
by the regularity of
\partial_{t}v_{0}
; hence, the evaluation of
v_{0}
in
t=0
is well-defined. Finally, (
4.19b
) is obtained by an integration by parts in time, using (
4.19a
) and the convergence properties of
(v_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
, since

\displaystyle-\left(v_{\varepsilon}(0),\varphi_{\varepsilon}(0)\right)_{{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\left\langle\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon},\varphi_{\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\left(v_{\varepsilon},\partial_{t}\varphi_{\varepsilon}\right)_{{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(4.34)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\langle\partial_{t}v_{0},\varphi\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\left(v_{\varepsilon},\partial_{t}\varphi_{\varepsilon}\right)_{{\mathcal{L}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=-\left(v_{0}(0),\varphi(0)\right)_{{\mathcal{L}}},

(4.35)

which concludes the proof.
∎

It should be noted that the regularity and uniform bounds on the time derivatives
\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}
are needed to obtain the time regularity of the limit function
v_{0}
, the convergence of the time derivatives in
(iii)
and the strong convergences in
(i)
— and consequently for the derivation of the coupling condition between
v_{0}^{\pm}
and
v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
on
\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm}
— but not for the derivation of the weak (two-scale) convergence of the function and its gradient in
(i)
and
(ii)
.

Moreover, the time regularity of the limit functions
v_{0}^{\dagger}
,
\dagger\in\{\pm,\,\mathrm{M}\}
, may be obtained directly by an argument similar to that used for the microsolutions
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\dagger}
at the end of
Subsection 3.2
.

Remark 4.4

(Regularity of
J_{0}
)
.

With
Theorem 4.3
at hand, we can gather significantly more information about the two-scale limit of
(J_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
. First of all, as the functions
J_{\varepsilon}
satisfy a priori estimates similar to (
4.9
) by
(T2)
, we have
J_{0}\coloneqq(1,J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}},1)\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}})
with
\partial_{t}J_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})
as well as
J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\in C^{0}([0,T];L^{2}(\Sigma\times Z_{*}))
. Moreover, we obtain
\partial_{t}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Sigma;(H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*}))^{\prime}))
and, due to the strong two-scale convergence of
(J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})_{\varepsilon}
to
J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
and the uniform boundedness of
({\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})\subset L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Sigma;W^{1,p}(Z_{*}))
, we further deduce
J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Sigma;W^{1,p}(Z_{*}))
for every
p\in[1,\infty)
and
J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\simeq 1
.

We also obtain the following convergence of the Jacobian at time
t=0
, and hence of the initial conditions.

Remark 4.5

(Convergence of
(J_{\varepsilon}(0))_{\varepsilon}
)
.

From
Theorem 4.3
we also have
J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}(0)\to J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}(0)
weakly in the two-scale sense. The strong two-scale convergence of
(J_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
combined with the continuity of both
J_{\varepsilon}
and
J_{0}
in
t=0
therefore allows to conclude
J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}(0)\to J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}(0)
strongly in the two-scale sense in every
L^{p}
–space,
p\in[1,\infty)
. By an application of
Lemma A.5
together with
(A3)
, we therefore also obtain
\tilde{U}_{j\varepsilon,0}^{\mathrm{M}}\to\tilde{U}_{j0,0}^{\mathrm{M}}
in the strong two-scale sense.

It is convenient to formulate the result of the application of
Theorem 4.3
to the sequence of microsolutions
(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
of (
3.11
) as a corollary.

Corollary 4.6

(Convergence of the microsolutions)
.

Let
(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
be the sequence of microsolutions of (
3.11
).
There exists a function
\tilde{u}_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}^{m})
with
\partial_{t}(J_{0}\tilde{u}_{0})\in L^{2}(0,T;({\mathcal{H}}^{m})^{\prime})
, and therefore
J_{0}\tilde{u}_{0}\in C^{0}([0,T];{\mathcal{L}}^{m})
, such that, for a subsequence, the following convergence properties hold true.

(i)

Convergence in the bulk.
The limits in the bulks satisfy

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rclcl}\chi_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}&\to&\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}&&\text{strongly in }L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\pm}),\\
\chi_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}&\to&\nabla\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}&&\text{weakly in }L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\pm}).\end{array}

(ii)

Convergence in the layer.
The limit in the layer satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rclcl}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}&\to&\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}&&\text{in the two-scale sense},\\
\varepsilon\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}&\to&\nabla_{z}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}&&\text{in the two-scale sense}.\end{array}

(iii)

Convergence of the time derivative.
For any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}([0,T);({\mathcal{H}}^{\infty})^{m})
and
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
defined as in (
(iii)
) we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{T}\left\langle\partial_{t}(J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}),\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\left\langle\partial_{t}(J_{0}\tilde{u}_{j0}),\varphi_{j}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t,

(4.40)

\displaystyle\left((J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon})(0),\varphi_{j\varepsilon}(0)\right)_{{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\to\left((J_{0}\tilde{u}_{j0})(0),\varphi_{j}(0)\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega^{+})\times L^{2}(\Sigma\times Z_{*})\times L^{2}(\Omega^{-})}.

(4.41)

Proof.

By the a priori estimates in (
3.13b
), choosing
v_{\varepsilon}=J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}
in
Theorem 4.3
, we directly obtain the existence of a limit function
v_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}^{m})
with
\partial_{t}v_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;({\mathcal{H}}^{m})^{\prime})
such that, for a subsequence, the convergence results in
(i)
and
4.40
are valid and
J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\to v_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}
holds in the two-scale sense.
Choosing
v_{\varepsilon}=\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}
in
Theorem 4.3
and using the a priori estimates (
3.13a
), we further deduce the existence of a limit function
\tilde{u}_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}^{m})
such that the convergences in
(ii)
hold, and are satisfied in
(i)
in the weak sense. It hence remains to identify
J_{0}\tilde{u}_{0}=v_{0}
, which then also yields the claimed time regularity of
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
.
This identification is due to the fact that we have
J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\to J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
strongly in the two-scale sense in any
L^{p}
,
p\in[1,\infty)
, and thus also for the inverse of the Jacobians. Indeed, from
Lemma A.5
we may now conclude
J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\to J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}
in the two-scale sense, and therefore
J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}=v_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
.
∎

We postpone a precise formulation of the time regularity of
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\dagger}
,
\dagger\in\{\pm,\,\mathrm{M}\}
to
Corollary 4.11
below as it is not needed for the derivation of the macroscopic problem in
Theorem 4.8
.

In order to pass to the limit in the nonlinearities, we need to establish strong two-scale convergence of the solutions
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
in the channel domain
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
. Making use of the unfolding operator
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}
(see
Definition A.2
) strong two-scale convergence of the sequence
(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})_{\varepsilon}
, can be established by showing strong
L^{2}
-convergence of the unfolded sequence
({\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})
, see
Lemma A.4
. The main compactness result we use to show this is given in
Theorem A.13
.

Lemma 4.7

(Strong convergence in the layer)
.

For every
p\in[1,2)
and
\beta\in(\tfrac{1}{2},1)
, the following converges hold true, possibly after passing to a subsequence,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rclcl}{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}&\to&\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}&&\text{ strongly in }L^{p}(\Sigma;L^{2}((0,T)\times Z_{*})),\\
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rvert_{N_{\varepsilon}})&\to&\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\rvert_{N}&&\text{ strongly in }L^{p}(\Sigma;L^{2}((0,T)\times N)),\\
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})&\to&J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}&&\text{ strongly in }L^{p}(\Sigma;L^{2}(0,T;H^{\beta}(Z_{*}))).\end{array}

Proof.

We establish the conditions
i)
and
ii)
of
Theorem A.13
for the sequence
(J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})
. The estimate
i)
is given by the a priori estimates in
Lemma 3.4
and uses (
3.141
).
The shifts of
J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
are controlled by
Lemma 3.6
, which implies
ii)
, where we use that
\lVert\delta_{l^{\prime},\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T;\widehat{\Omega}_{\varepsilon,h}^{\pm})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\to 0
as
|\varepsilon l^{\prime}|\to 0
due to (
(i)
), and
(S3)
.

Theorem A.13
thus allows us to find a function
v_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Sigma;H^{1}(Z_{*})^{m}))
such that, for a subsequence,
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})\to v_{j0}
strongly in
L^{p}(\Sigma;L^{2}(0,T;H^{\beta}(Z_{*})^{m}))
, and using
Corollary 4.6

(ii)
,
Lemma A.5
and the strong two-scale convergence
J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\to J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
, we conclude
v_{0}=J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
. Therefore, the last convergence in (
4.7
) is obtained.
The continuity of the embeddings
H^{\beta}(Z_{*})\xhookrightarrow{}L^{2}(Z_{*})
and
H^{\beta}(Z_{*})\xhookrightarrow{}L^{2}(N)
further gives
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})\to J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}
strongly in
L^{p}(\Sigma;L^{2}((0,T)\times Z_{*}))
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rvert_{N_{\varepsilon}})\to J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\rvert_{N}
strongly in
L^{p}(\Sigma;L^{2}((0,T)\times N))
.
A direct calculation using
J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}},J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\sim 1
, the strong two-scale convergence of
(J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})_{\varepsilon}
in every
L^{q}
,
q\in(1,\infty)
and Hölder’s inequality thus yields
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\to\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}
in
L^{p}(\Sigma;L^{2}((0,T)\times Z_{*}))
.
The second convergence in (
4.7
) is a consequence of Pratt’s theorem (see
[
Pra60
]
) and uses the strong convergence of
(J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})_{\varepsilon}
in
L^{p}(\Sigma;L^{2}(0,T;H^{\beta}(Z_{*})))
, which holds due to
Theorem A.13
and the assumptions
(T1)
and
(T2)
together with the continuity of the trace embedding
H^{\beta}(Z_{*})\xhookrightarrow{}L^{2}(\partial Z_{*})
.
∎

4.2
Identification of the macroscopic reference model

We next derive the limit problem which the function
\tilde{u}_{0}=(\tilde{u}_{0}^{+},\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}},\tilde{u}_{0}^{-})
satisfies and discuss some of its properties.
We write
F_{0}\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{z}\psi_{0}
and define, using the same notation as in
(A1)
–
(A3)
,

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}=F_{0}^{-1}\bar{D}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}F_{0}^{-\top},\qquad\tilde{q}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}=F_{0}^{-1}\bar{q}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}},\qquad\tilde{U}_{0,0}=U_{0,0}\circ\psi_{0}(0,\cdot),\qquad\tilde{b}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}=F_{0}^{-1}\partial_{t}\psi_{0}.

(4.45)

First, we pass to the limit in the microscopic formulation (
3.9
), in which we denote the outer unit normal of
Z_{*}
by
\nu^{\mathrm{M}}
.

Theorem 4.8

(Macroscopic transformed problem)
.

The limit function
\tilde{u}_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}^{m})
satisfies
\partial_{t}(J_{0}\tilde{u}_{0})\in L^{2}(0,T;({\mathcal{H}}^{m})^{\prime})
and for every
\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}}^{m}
and almost every
t\in(0,T)
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\langle\partial_{t}(J_{0}\tilde{u}_{j0}),\varphi_{j}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}}+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{D}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla_{z}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla_{z}\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\\
&\quad-\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{q}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla_{z}\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}+\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{b}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla_{z}\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\\
&=\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}f_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm})\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}g_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}-\int_{\Sigma}\int_{N}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\lVert F_{0}^{-\top}\nu^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert{h}_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\end{split}

(4.46)

together with the initial conditions
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm}=\tilde{U}_{0}^{\pm}
a.e. in
\Omega^{\pm}
and
(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})(0)=J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}(0)\tilde{U}_{0,0}^{\mathrm{M}}
a.e. in
\Sigma\times Z_{*}
.

Proof.

The regularity of
\tilde{u}_{0}
is proven in
Corollary 4.6
. Given
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T);({\mathcal{H}}^{\infty})^{m})
, we consider test functions
\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T);({\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon})^{m})
of the form (
(iii)
) in the weak formulation (
3.9
) to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left\langle\partial_{t}(J_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}),\varphi_{j\varepsilon}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}\left(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}-\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm}\right)\cdot(\nabla\varphi_{j}^{\pm})_{\varepsilon}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(4.47)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}\left(\varepsilon J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{D}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}-J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{q}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{b}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\right)\cdot\left((\nabla_{x^{\prime}}\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}})_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\nabla_{z}\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}})_{\varepsilon}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(4.48)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}f_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})\varphi_{j\varepsilon}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}g_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(4.49)

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{N_{\varepsilon}}J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\lVert F_{\varepsilon}^{-\top}\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert h_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t,

(4.50)

where
(\nabla\varphi^{\pm})_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\nabla\varphi^{\pm}(t,x^{\prime},x_{n}\mp\varepsilon)
,
(\nabla_{x^{\prime}}\varphi^{\mathrm{M}})_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\nabla_{x^{\prime}}\varphi^{\mathrm{M}}(t,x^{\prime},\tfrac{x_{n}}{\varepsilon})
and similarly for
(\nabla_{z}\varphi^{\mathrm{M}})_{\varepsilon}
.
The limit of the integrals over
\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}
is obtained using the convergence results from
Corollary 4.6

(i)
together with
(\nabla\varphi_{j}^{\pm})_{\varepsilon}\to\nabla\varphi_{j}^{\pm}
in
L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\pm})^{n}
.
By
Lemma A.5
as well as the assumptions
(T4)
and
(A1)
–
(A3)
, we have
\tilde{D}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\to\tilde{D}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}
,
\tilde{q}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\to\tilde{q}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}
,
\tilde{b}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\to\tilde{b}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
, and
\tilde{U}_{j\varepsilon,0}^{\mathrm{M}}\to\tilde{U}_{j0,0}^{\mathrm{M}}
in the strong two-scale sense. Therefore, due to the convergence results in
Corollary 4.6

(ii)
, products of the data and
J_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
with the functions
\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
and
\varepsilon\nabla\tilde{u}_{j\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}
converge in the two-scale sense, and we have that
\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}
and
\nabla_{z}\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}
are admissible test functions to pass to the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
in the integrals over
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
in the previous equation.
The convergence of the generalised time derivative is obtained by
Corollary 4.6

(iii)
.
Moreover, due to the Lipschitz continuity of the reaction rates in the last argument, the strong (two-scale) convergence of
(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\dagger})_{\varepsilon}
and
Lemma A.6
, for
p\in[1,2)
we have

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rclcl}\chi_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}f_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\pm})&\to&f_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm})&&\text{strongly in }L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\pm}),\\
g_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})&\to&g_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})&&\text{strongly in the two-scale sense in $L^{p}$,}\\
\lVert F_{\varepsilon}^{-\top}\nu_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert h_{j}(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})&\to&\lVert F_{0}^{-\top}\nu^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert h_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})&&\text{strongly in the two-scale sense on $N_{\varepsilon}$ in $L^{p}$.}\end{array}

In total, along a subsequence we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{T}\left\langle\partial_{t}(J_{0}\tilde{u}_{j0}),\varphi_{j}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}\left(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}-\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm}\right)\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}\left(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{D}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla_{z}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}-J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{q}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}+J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{b}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\right)\cdot\nabla_{z}\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}f_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm})\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}g_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\\
&\qquad-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{N}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\lVert F_{0}^{-\top}\nu^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert h_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

(4.54)

By density, (
4.54
) holds true for all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T);{\mathcal{H}}^{m})
. In particular, choosing test functions with separated
t
–dependency, we deduce (
4.46
). The initial conditions are a direct consequence of (
4.41
) together with
(A3)
.
∎

Remark 4.9

(Limit passage in (
3.9
))
.

A more standard way to pass to the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
in (
3.9
) and to conclude the regularity of
\partial_{t}(J_{0}\tilde{u}_{0})
is by integrating by parts in time in the weak microscopic formulation and taking the limit in the resulting terms separately. However, when dealing with test functions
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}([0,T);{\mathcal{H}}^{\infty})^{m}
, and without having a result like (
4.41
) at hand, it is a priori not clear why
\left(J_{\varepsilon}(0)\tilde{U}_{j\varepsilon,0},\varphi_{j\varepsilon}(0)\right)_{{\mathcal{L}}_{\varepsilon}}\to\left(J_{0}(0)\tilde{U}_{j0,0},\varphi_{j}(0)\right)_{{\mathcal{L}}}
holds in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Corollary 4.10

(Convergence of the entire sequence)
.

The function
\tilde{u}_{0}
is the unique weak solution of the macroscopic problem (
4.46
). In particular, the convergence result from
Corollary 4.6
holds true for the entire sequence
(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

The uniqueness of weak solutions is obtained similarly as for the microscopic problem in
Lemma 3.5
by an application of Gronwall’s inequality, using that the limit data
\tilde{D}_{0}
,
\tilde{q}_{0}
and
\tilde{b}_{0}
satisfy similar estimates as the microscopic ones.
In particular, the entire sequence
(\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
converges to the solution
\tilde{u}_{0}
of the macroscopic problem (
4.46
).
∎

Next, we aim to provide more insight into the individual problems solved by the functions
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm}
and
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
, which, in a final step, yields macroscopic problems on the bulk domains
\Omega^{\pm}
coupled by effective transmission conditions given in terms of local cell problems stated on the standard cell
Z_{*}
.

Corollary 4.11

(Macroscopic transformed problem and local cell problems)
.

For the limit function
\tilde{u}_{0}=(\tilde{u}_{0}^{+},\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}},\tilde{u}_{0}^{-})
, there holds

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega^{\pm})^{m})\quad\text{with}\quad\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm}\in L^{2}(0,T;(H^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega^{\pm})^{m})^{\prime})

(4.55)

as well as
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm}(0)=\tilde{U}_{0}^{\pm}
a.e. in
\Omega^{\pm}
and
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm}=\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
on
(0,T)\times\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm}
.
Further, for every
\varphi^{\pm}\in H^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega^{\pm})^{m}
and almost every
t\in(0,T)
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm},\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\right\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega^{\pm}))^{\prime},\,H^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega^{\pm})}+\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}\left(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}-\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm}\right)\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}f_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm})\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

(4.56)

Here, the function

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Sigma;H^{1}(Z_{*})^{m}))\quad\text{with}\quad\partial_{t}(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Sigma;(H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*})^{m})^{\prime}))

(4.57)

is such that
(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})(0)=J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}(0)\tilde{U}_{0,0}^{\mathrm{M}}
a.e. in
\Sigma\times Z_{*}
; moreover, the local cell problem is given by: For every
\varphi^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{2}(\Sigma;H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*})^{m})
and almost every
t\in(0,T)

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}),\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma;(H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*}))^{\prime}),\,L^{2}(\Sigma;H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*}))}+\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{D}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla_{z}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla_{z}\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

(4.58)

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{q}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla_{z}\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}+\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{b}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla_{z}\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

(4.59)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}g_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}-\int_{\Sigma}\int_{N}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\lVert F_{0}^{-\top}\nu^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert{h}_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}.

(4.60)

Proof.

Using the Gelfand triples
H^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega^{\pm})\xhookrightarrow{}L^{2}(\Omega^{\pm})\xhookrightarrow{}(H^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega^{\pm}))^{\prime}
and
L^{2}(\Sigma;H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*}))\xhookrightarrow{}L^{2}(\Sigma\times Z_{*})\xhookrightarrow{}L^{2}(\Sigma;(H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*}))^{\prime})
, for all
\Phi^{\pm},\,\Phi^{\mathrm{M}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{m}
of the form
\Phi^{+}=(\varphi^{+},0,0)
,
\Phi^{\mathrm{M}}=(0,\varphi^{\mathrm{M}},0)
and
\Phi^{-}=(0,0,\varphi^{-})
, where
\varphi^{\pm}\in H^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega^{\pm})^{m}
and
\varphi^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{2}(\Sigma;H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*})^{m})
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm},\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\right\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega^{\pm}))^{\prime},\,H^{1}_{\Sigma,0}(\Omega^{\pm})}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\partial_{t}(J_{0}\tilde{u}_{j0}),\Phi_{j}^{\pm}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}},

(4.61)

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}),\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma;(H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*}))^{\prime}),\,L^{2}(\Sigma;H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*}))}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\partial_{t}(J_{0}\tilde{u}_{j0}),\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},\,{\mathcal{H}}}.

(4.62)

This immediately yields the claimed time regularity of
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\dagger}
; choosing
\Phi^{\pm},\,\Phi^{\mathrm{M}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{m}
as test functions in (
4.46
) the proof is complete.
∎

In contrast to the microsolutions
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
, the limit function
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
is not continuous in time, that is
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\notin C^{0}([0,T];L^{2}(\Sigma\times Z_{*}))
in general, as the following remark shows. In particular, the initial condition
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}=\tilde{U}_{0,0}^{\mathrm{M}}
a.e. in
\Sigma\times Z_{*}
is ill-posed.

Remark 4.12

(Regularity of
\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
and initial condition of
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
)
.

Due to the low regularity of
\partial_{t}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
, see
Remark 4.4
, we only have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}=(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})^{-1}\partial_{t}(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})-(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})^{-1}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\partial_{t}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{1}((0,T)\times\Sigma;(W^{1,q}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*})^{m})^{\prime})

(4.63)

for every
q>n
(see
[
GNP21
, p. 122]
for a similar argument on bulk domains). Indeed, for every
\varphi\in H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*})
and almost every
(t,x^{\prime})\in(0,T)\times\Sigma
, there holds
(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})^{-1}(t,x^{\prime},\cdot)\varphi\in H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*})
using that
J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Sigma;W^{1,p}(Z_{*}))
for every
p\geq 1
together with the embeddings
W^{1,p}(Z_{*})\xhookrightarrow{}L^{\infty}(Z_{*})
for
p>n
and
H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*})\xhookrightarrow{}L^{2^{*}}(Z_{*})
as well as the generalised Hölder inequality. Therefore, the first term in (
4.63
) belongs to
L^{2}((0,T)\times\Sigma;(H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*})^{m})^{\prime})
. By a similar argument, for every
\varphi\in W^{1,q}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*})
with
q>n
, we have
(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})^{-1}(t,x^{\prime},\cdot)\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}(t,x^{\prime},\cdot)\varphi\in H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*})^{m}
; hence, the second term in (
4.63
) belongs to
L^{1}((0,T)\times\Sigma;(W^{1,q}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*})^{m})^{\prime})
. We stress that the additional requirement
\partial_{t}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
would allow to obtain
\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\Sigma;(W^{1,q}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*})^{m})^{\prime})
and, therefore, also
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\in C^{0}([0,T]\times\overline{\Sigma};L^{2}(Z_{*})^{m})
.

Nevertheless, we emphasise that by rephrasing the argument given in
[
GNP21
, p. 123]
, there exists a set
N\subset(0,T)
of measure zero such that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0,\,t\notin N}\lVert\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}(t)-\tilde{U}_{0,0}^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert_{\smash{L^{1}(\Sigma\times Z)}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}=0,

(4.64)

which gives a weak formulation of the initial condition for
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
.

Remark 4.13

(Strong formulation of the macroscopic transformed problem)
.

The strong problem associated with the weak formulation in
Corollary 4.11
reads as follows. The limit functions
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm}
solve

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\ }c@{\ \, }l}\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}-\nabla\cdot(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}-\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm})&=&f_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm})&&\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega^{\pm},\\
(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}-\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm})\cdot\nu^{\pm}&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial\Omega^{\pm}\setminus\Sigma,\\
\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}&=&\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm},\end{array}\right.

with initial condition
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm}(0)=\tilde{U}_{0}^{\pm}
in
\Omega^{\pm}
, whereas
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
with initial condition
(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})(0)=J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}(0)\tilde{U}_{0,0}^{\mathrm{M}}
in
\Sigma\times Z_{*}
is a solution of the local cell problems

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\ }c@{\ \, }l}\partial_{t}(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}})-\nabla_{z}\cdot(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{D}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla_{z}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}-J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{q}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}+J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{b}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})&=&J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}g_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})&&\text{in }(0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*},\\
-(\tilde{D}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla_{z}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}-\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{q}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}+\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{b}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\cdot\nu^{\mathrm{M}}&=&\lVert F_{0}^{-\top}\nu^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert h_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\Sigma\times N.\end{array}\right.

We next show that under additional regularity assumptions on the time derivatives
\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\dagger}
we have an effective jump condition for the total fluxes on
S_{*}^{\pm}
in a weak sense. For a Lipschitz domain
U\subset{\mathbbm{R}}^{n}
with outer unit normal
\nu
, we denote by
H^{1}_{\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\nolimits}(U)
the space of all functions in
L^{2}(U)^{n}
whose distributional divergence belongs to
L^{2}(U)
. Then, there exists a linear and bounded operator
\gamma_{\nu}\colon H^{1}_{\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\nolimits}(U)\to H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial U)
, called the normal trace operator, such that for all
u\in C^{\infty}(\overline{U})^{n}
we have
\gamma_{\nu}(u)=u\rvert_{\partial U}\cdot\nu
and

\displaystyle\left\langle\gamma_{\nu}(u),\varphi\right\rangle_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial U),\,H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial U)}=\int_{U}(u\cdot\nabla\varphi+\varphi\nabla\cdot u)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{for all }u\in H^{1}_{\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\nolimits}(U),\,\varphi\in H^{1}(U),

(4.70)

see
[
SS92
, Theorem 5.3]
.
In what follows,
\gamma_{\nu^{\pm}}
resp.
\gamma_{\nu^{*}}
denote the normal trace operators associated with
\Omega^{\pm}
resp.
Z_{*}
.

Lemma 4.14

(Effective transmission condition for the total flux)
.

Assume that
\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\pm})^{m}
and
\partial_{t}(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})^{m}
. Then, for almost every
t\in(0,T)
, the total fluxes

\displaystyle F_{j}^{\pm}=D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}-\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm}\qquad\text{and}\qquad F_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}=J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}D_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}-J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{q}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}+J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{b}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}

(4.71)

satisfy
F_{j}^{\pm}\in H^{1}_{\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\nolimits}(\Omega^{\pm})
,
F_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{2}(\Sigma;H^{1}_{\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\nolimits}(Z_{*}))
, and for every
\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}}^{m}
we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\gamma_{\nu^{\pm}}(F_{j}^{\pm}),\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\right\rangle_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega^{\pm}),\,H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega^{\pm})}

(4.72)

\displaystyle\quad=-\int_{\Sigma}\left\langle\gamma_{\nu^{*}}(F_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}),\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial Z_{*}),\,H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial Z_{*})}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}-\int_{\Sigma}\int_{N}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\lVert F_{0}^{-\top}\nu^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert{h}_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}.

(4.73)

Proof.

Under the given regularity of the time derivatives
\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\dagger}
,
\dagger\in\{\pm,\,\mathrm{M}\}
, choosing test functions
\varphi^{\pm}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\Omega^{\pm})^{m}
resp.
\varphi^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{2}(\Sigma;C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(Z_{*})^{m})
in the weak macroscopic formulations obtained in
Corollary 4.11
, for almost every
t\in(0,T)
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}F_{j}^{\pm}\in H^{1}_{\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\nolimits}(\Omega^{\pm})&\text{with}&\nabla\cdot F_{j}^{\pm}=\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}-f_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm}),\\
F_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\in L^{2}(\Sigma;H^{1}_{\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\nolimits}(Z_{*}))&\text{with}&\nabla\cdot F_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}=\partial_{t}(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}})-g_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}).\end{array}

Using
(\varphi^{+},\varphi^{\mathrm{M}},0)\in{\mathcal{H}}^{m}
(resp.
(0,\varphi^{\mathrm{M}},\varphi^{-})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{m}
) as a test function in (
4.46
), by the generalised divergence theorem we deduce

\displaystyle\left\langle\gamma_{\nu^{\pm}}(F_{j}^{\pm}),\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\right\rangle_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega^{\pm}),\,H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega^{\pm})}

(4.76)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}F_{j}^{\pm}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}(\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}-f_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm}))\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(4.77)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}(\partial_{t}(J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}})-J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}g_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}))\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}-\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}F_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

(4.78)

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\Sigma}\int_{N}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\lVert F_{0}^{-\top}\nu^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert{h}_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

(4.79)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma}\left\langle\gamma_{\nu^{*}}(F_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}),\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial Z_{*}),\,H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial Z_{*})}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}-\int_{\Sigma}\int_{N}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\lVert F_{0}^{-\top}\nu^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert{h}_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime},

(4.80)

and the proof is complete.
∎

Higher regularity of the time derivatives
\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{0}^{\dagger}
, as it was assumed right from the beginning in
[
NJ07
]
, may be obtained by increasing the regularity of the data, see e.g.
[
Lio61
, Chapter V, Theorem 6.1]
. Next, for sufficiently smooth solutions
\tilde{u}_{0}
we aim to give further insight into the transmission condition obtained in
Lemma 4.14
by a similar choice of test functions as in
[
NJ07
]
.

Remark 4.15

(Effective jump conditions for the total fluxes)
.

Given
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((-H,H))
with
\psi(x_{n})=1
for
|x_{n}|<\frac{H}{2}
, we define

\displaystyle\left\langle\gamma_{\nu^{\pm}}\rvert_{\Sigma}(F_{j}^{\pm}),\eta_{j}\right\rangle_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma),\,H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma)}\coloneqq\left\langle\gamma_{\nu^{\pm}}(F_{j}^{\pm}),\psi\eta_{j}\right\rangle_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega^{\pm}),\,H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega^{\pm})}

(4.81)

for every
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\Sigma)^{m}
. Thus, taking the
z
–independent test function
\varphi=\psi\eta
in
Lemma 4.14
, we deduce

\displaystyle\left\langle\gamma_{\nu^{\pm}}\rvert_{\Sigma}(F_{j}^{\pm}),\eta_{j}\right\rangle_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma),\,H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma)}

(4.82)

\displaystyle\quad=-\int_{\Sigma}\left\langle\gamma_{\nu^{*}}(F_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}),1\right\rangle_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial Z_{*}),\,H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial Z_{*})}\,\eta_{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}-\int_{\Sigma}\int_{N}J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\lVert F_{0}^{-\top}\nu^{\mathrm{M}}\rVert{h}_{j}(\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(z)\eta_{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}.

(4.83)

In particular, for a sufficiently regular solution
\tilde{u}_{0}
of (
4.46
), the macroscopic total flux
F_{j}^{\pm}
at
\Sigma
equals the averaged microscopic flux
F_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}
over the top (resp. bottom) of the standard cell, that is

\displaystyle(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}-\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm})\cdot n^{\pm}=\int_{S_{*}^{\pm}}(\tilde{D}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}-\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{q}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}})\cdot n^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(z)\qquad\text{on }(0,T)\times\Sigma,

(4.84)

where
n^{\pm}=(0^{\prime},\pm 1)\in{\mathbbm{R}}^{n}
, and
J_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\equiv 1
on
(0,T)\times\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm}
and the boundary condition on
N
for
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
have been used. This interface condition for the total fluxes thus complements the condition
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\pm}=\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
on
(0,T)\times\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm}
for the solutions, where we stress that it implies that
\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\rvert_{S_{*}^{\pm}}(\cdot_{t},\cdot_{x^{\prime}},z)
is independent of
z\in S_{*}^{\pm}
. Similarly, the jump of the total fluxes across
(0,T)\times\Sigma
is given by

\displaystyle(D_{j}^{+}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j0}^{+}-\tilde{u}_{j0}^{+}q_{j}^{+}-(D_{j}^{-}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j0}^{-}-\tilde{u}_{j0}^{-}q_{j}^{-}))\cdot n^{+}=\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{S_{*}^{\pm}}(\tilde{D}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}-\tilde{u}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{q}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}})\cdot n^{+}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(z).

(4.85)

4.3
Formulation of the macroscopic model with cell problems evolving in time

In a last step, we formulate the macroscopic problem (
4.46
) on the macroscopic domains
\Omega^{\pm}
but with local cell problems posed on cells evolving in time and depending additionally on
\Sigma
. More precisely, for
(t,x^{\prime})\in(0,T)\times\Sigma
, we define
Z_{*}(t,x^{\prime})\coloneqq\psi_{0}(t,x^{\prime},Z_{*})
and
N(t,x^{\prime})\coloneqq\psi_{0}(t,x^{\prime},N)
, and we write

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{T,*}^{\mathrm{M}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{(t,x^{\prime},z)\,\colon\,t\in(0,T),\,x^{\prime}\in\Sigma,\,z\in Z_{*}(t,x^{\prime})\right\},

(4.86)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}_{T}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{(t,x,z)\,\colon\,t\in(0,T),\,x^{\prime}\in\Sigma,\,z\in N(t,x^{\prime})\right\}.

(4.87)

Then, denoting
\psi_{0}^{-1}(t,x^{\prime},\cdot)\coloneqq\psi_{0}(t,x^{\prime},\cdot)^{-1}
, we write
u_{0}=(\tilde{u}_{0}^{+},\tilde{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\circ_{z}\psi_{0}^{-1},\tilde{u}_{0}^{-})
and define the macroscopic quantities

\displaystyle D_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\coloneqq\bar{D}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\circ_{z}\psi_{0}^{-1},\qquad q_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\coloneqq\bar{q}_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\circ_{z}\psi_{0}^{-1},\qquad b_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}\coloneqq\partial_{t}\psi_{0}\circ_{z}\psi_{0}^{-1},\qquad U_{j0,0}^{\mathrm{M}}\coloneqq\tilde{U}_{j0,0}^{\mathrm{M}}\circ_{z}\psi_{0}^{-1}(0,\cdot)

(4.88)

on the evolving macroscopic layer
{\mathcal{Q}}_{T,*}^{\mathrm{M}}
. We further recall that, by the assumptions on the microscopic transformation
\psi_{\varepsilon}
, we have
\psi_{0}(t,\cdot)\rvert_{\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm}}=\mathrm{id}_{\Sigma\times Z_{*}}
; hence, the top and bottom of the evolving channel
Z_{*}(t,x^{\prime})
in
Z
is again given by
S_{*}^{\pm}
. The reverse transformation
\psi_{0}^{-1}
applied to the weak macroscopic problem in
Corollary 4.11
yields the following weak equation on the macroscopic domains
\Omega^{\pm}
, coupled with an equation on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{T,*}^{\mathrm{M}}
.

Corollary 4.16

(Evolving macroscopic problem)
.

The function
u_{0}
satisfies

\displaystyle u_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;[H^{1}(\Omega^{+})^{m}\times L^{2}(\Sigma;H^{1}(Z_{*}(t,x^{\prime}))^{m})\times H^{1}(\Omega^{-})^{m}])

(4.89)

and
u_{0}^{\pm}=u_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
on
(0,T)\times\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm}
, and for all
\varphi^{\pm}\in C^{1}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega^{\pm}})^{m}
and
\varphi^{\mathrm{M}}\in C^{1}([0,T]\times\overline{\Sigma}\times\overline{Z})^{m}
with
\varphi^{\mathrm{M}}=\varphi^{\pm}
on
(0,T)\times\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm}
and
\varphi^{\pm}(T,\cdot)=\varphi^{\mathrm{M}}(T,\cdot)=0
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&-\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}u_{j0}^{\pm}\partial_{t}\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}(t,x^{\prime})}u_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\partial_{t}\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla u_{j0}^{\pm}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}(t,x^{\prime})}D_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla_{z}u_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla_{z}\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad-\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}q_{j}^{\pm}u_{j0}^{\pm}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}(t,x^{\prime})}u_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}q_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\cdot\nabla_{z}\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}f_{j}(u_{0}^{\pm})\varphi_{j}^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}(t,x^{\prime})}g_{j}(u_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{N(t,x^{\prime})}h_{j}(u_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad+\mathop{\smash{\sum_{\pm}}}\displaylimits\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}U_{j0}^{\pm}\varphi_{j}^{\pm}(0)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}(0,x^{\prime})}U_{j0,0}^{\mathrm{M}}\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{M}}(0)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}.\end{split}

(4.90)

Proof.

As
\psi_{0}(t,\cdot)\rvert_{\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm}}=\mathrm{id}_{\Sigma\times Z_{*}}
, the functions
\tilde{\varphi}=(\varphi^{+},\varphi^{\mathrm{M}}\circ_{z}\psi_{0},\varphi^{-})\in L^{2}(0,T;{\mathcal{H}}^{m})
, with
\varphi^{\pm},\,\varphi^{\mathrm{M}}
as above, are admissible in the weak formulation (
4.46
). Therefore, by applying the inverse transformation
\psi_{0}^{-1}
and using transformation rules similar to that employed in
Section 3
for the derivation of the weak microscopic problem on the reference domain, the result is obtained.
∎

We emphasise that information on the microscopic evolution of the channels is captured in the two-scale limit transformation
\psi_{0}
, and hence in the evolving microcells
Z_{*}(t,x^{\prime})
for
(t,x^{\prime})\in(0,T)\times\Sigma
. The strong macroscopic problems on
\Omega^{\pm}
are therefore coupled by local cell problems on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{T,*}^{\mathrm{M}}
taking into account this evolution.

Remark 4.17

(Strong formulation of the macroscopic problem)
.

The weak formulation (
4.90
) is associated with the problem

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\ }c@{\ \, }l}\partial_{t}u_{j0}^{\pm}-\nabla\cdot(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla u_{j0}^{\pm}-q_{j}^{\pm}u_{j0}^{\pm})&=&f_{j}(u_{0}^{\pm})&&\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega^{\pm},\\
(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla u_{j0}^{\pm}-q_{j}^{\pm}u_{j0}^{\pm})\cdot\nu^{\pm}&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial\Omega^{\pm}\setminus\Sigma,\\
u_{0}^{\pm}(0)&=&{U}_{0}^{\pm}&&\text{in }\Omega^{\pm},\end{array}\right.

where the function
u_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}
solves for
(t,x^{\prime})\in(0,T)\times\Sigma
the local cell problems on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{T,*}^{\mathrm{M}}
given by

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\ }c@{\ \, }l}\partial_{t}u_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}-\nabla_{z}\cdot(D_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla_{z}u_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}-u_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}q_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}})&=&g_{j}(u_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})&&\text{in }{\mathcal{Q}}_{T,*}^{\mathrm{M}},\\
-(D_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla_{z}u_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}-u_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}q_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}+u_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}b_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})\cdot\nu^{\mathrm{M}}&=&h_{j}(u_{0}^{\mathrm{M}})&&\text{on }{\mathcal{N}}_{T},\\
u_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}(0)&=&U_{0,0}^{\mathrm{M}}&&\text{in }\left\{(x^{\prime},z)\,\colon\,x^{\prime}\in\Sigma,\,z\in Z_{*}(0,x^{\prime})\right\}.\end{array}\right.

The systems (
4.91
) and (
4.91
) are coupled by the transmission conditions

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\ }c@{\ \, }l}u_{0}^{\pm}&=&u_{0}^{\mathrm{M}}&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\Sigma\times S_{*}^{\pm},\\
\left(D_{j}^{\pm}\nabla u_{j0}^{\pm}-u_{j0}^{\pm}q_{j}^{\pm}\right)\cdot n^{\pm}&=&\int_{S_{*}^{\pm}}\left(D_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\nabla u_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}-u_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}q_{j0}^{\mathrm{M}}\right)\cdot n^{\pm}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(z)&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\Sigma,\end{array}\right.

which give rise to jump conditions for the flux across the hypersurface
\Sigma
.

5
Conclusion

We derived a macroscopic model for a coupled system of reaction–diffusion–advection equations posed on two bulk domains connected via channels within a thin layer. The microscopic (channel) structure evolves in time and its evolution is known a priori. We considered nonlinear (boundary) reaction rates as well as a critical, low diffusivity of order
\varepsilon
inside the layer, causing the limit function to depend on both the macroscopic and the microscopic variable. The resulting macroscopic equations on the bulk regions are coupled through effective transmission conditions on the interface
\Sigma
, expressed in terms of local cell problems on a space–time-dependent reference cell
Z_{*}(t,x^{\prime})
. This reference cell depends on the two-scale limit of the microscopic evolution, with the macroscopic variable
x^{\prime}\in\Sigma
acting as a parameter, and thus encodes information on the evolving microstructure.
The macroscopic problem was obtained by first proving an abstract (two-scale) convergence results for sequences taking values in an appropriately scaled function space and then identifying the system of equations satisfied by the limit functions, with all convergence properties derived solely from a priori estimates for the solutions of the microscopic problem.

In modelling applications, the evolution of the microstructure is often not known a priori but determined by different physical, chemical or mechanical processes taking place on the lateral boundary of the channels. This leads to a strongly coupled nonlinear system including free boundaries, which provides an interesting potential extension of our analysis. Besides having to deal with the nonlinear coupling, a suitable construction of the microscopic transformation depending on the processes on the channel walls is an essential challenge in this task; nevertheless, the results obtained in this work provide a firm basis for such an undertaking.

Appendix A
Two-scale convergence for (non-evolving) thin channels

In this appendix, we summarise the most important results on two-scale convergence in (non-evolving) thin channels (see
Definition 4.1
) using the notation introduced in
Subsection 3.2
and
Subsection 4.1
. We mostly skip the proofs but give references to the literature.

A.1
Two-scale convergence and periodic unfolding

The following lemma collects compactness properties of (weak) two-scale convergence based on a priori bounds, which for simplicity we only state for the case
p=2
. Partial results for
p\in(1,\infty)
are given in
[
GN21
, Lemma 3.2]
.

Lemma A.1

(Two-scale compactness)
.

(i)

If
v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})
satisfies
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\lVert v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1
, there exists
v_{0}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
such that, for a subsequence,
v_{\varepsilon}\to v_{0}
in the two-scale sense.

(ii)

If
v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times N_{\varepsilon})
satisfies
\lVert v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}((0,T)\times N_{\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1
, there exists
v_{0}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times N)
such that, for a subsequence,
v_{\varepsilon}\to v_{0}
in the two-scale sense on
N_{\varepsilon}
.

(iii)

If
v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}))
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\lVert v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lVert\nabla v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1,

(A.1)

there exists
v_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Sigma;H^{1}(Z_{*})))
such that, for a subsequence,
v_{\varepsilon}\to v_{0}
and
\varepsilon\nabla v_{\varepsilon}\to\nabla_{z}v_{0}
in the two-scale sense.

(iv)

If
v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}))
with
\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;({\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon,0}^{\mathrm{M}})^{\prime})
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\lVert\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}(0,T;({\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon,0}^{\mathrm{M}})^{\prime})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\lVert v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lVert\nabla v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1,

(A.2)

there exists
v_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Sigma;H^{1}(Z_{*})))
with
\partial_{t}v_{0}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Sigma;(H^{1}_{\pm,0}(Z_{*}))^{\prime})
such that, for a subsequence,
v_{\varepsilon}\to v_{0}
and
\varepsilon\nabla v_{\varepsilon}\to\nabla_{z}v_{0}
in the two-scale sense.

Proof.

The compactness results
(i)
and
(ii)
are stated in
[
BGN22
, Theorem 4.4]
without proof as it is similar to
[
NJ07
, Proposition 4.2]
. Note that the latter one is based on an oscillation lemma on the lateral boundary, see
[
BGN22
, Lemma 4.3]
. The proof of
(iii)
can be found in
[
GN21
, Lemma 3.2]
.
The proof of
(iv)
is similar as in
[
GN25
, Lemma B.5]
(also see
[
GNP21
, Proposition 4]
for the case of bulk regions and
Theorem 4.3
) by adjusting the space of test functions accordingly.
∎

Next, we introduce the unfolding operator
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}
for thin channels which allows to establish (strong) two-scale convergence of a sequence
(v_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
by showing (strong)
L^{p}
–convergence of the unfolded sequence
({\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
, see
Lemma A.4
below.

Definition A.2

(Unfolding operator,
[
GN21
, Definition 3.3]
)
.

Let
(G_{\varepsilon},G)\in\{(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon},Z_{*}),(N_{\varepsilon},N)\}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
. The
unfolding operator

{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}\colon L^{p}((0,T)\times G_{\varepsilon})\to L^{p}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times G)
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}(t,x^{\prime},z)\coloneqq v_{\varepsilon}\left(t,\varepsilon\left(\left\lfloor\tfrac{x^{\prime}}{\varepsilon}\right\rfloor,0\right)+\varepsilon z\right)\qquad\text{for }(t,x^{\prime},z)\in(0,T)\times\Sigma\times G,

(A.3)

where
\left\lfloor\cdot\right\rfloor
denotes the integer part.

We emphasise that time only acts as a parameter in the preceding definition of the unfolding operator, hence the following results are also valid in the stationary case. As mentioned in
[
GN21
, p. 1588]
, for functions in
L^{p}(0,T;W^{1,p}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}))
it makes sense not to distinguish the notation of the unfolding operator on
\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}
and on
N_{\varepsilon}
as it commutes with the trace operator.
We have the following important properties of
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}
, see
[
NJ07
, Lemma 4.6]
and
[
GN21
, Lemma 3.4]
as well as
[
CDG18
, Chapter 1]
for similar results in the case of bulk domains.

Lemma A.3

(Properties of
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}
)
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty)
.

(i)

For any
v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{p}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})
, we have
\lVert{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{p}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}=\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{p}}\lVert v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{p}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}
.

(ii)

For any
v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{p}((0,T)\times N_{\varepsilon})
, we have
\lVert{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{p}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times N)}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}=\lVert v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{p}((0,T)\times N_{\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}
.

(iii)

For any
v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{p}(0,T;W^{1,p}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}))
, we have
\nabla_{z}({\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon})=\varepsilon{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(\nabla_{x}v_{\varepsilon})
.

Lemma A.4

(Characterisation of two-scale convergence)
.

Let
(G_{\varepsilon},G)\in\{(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon},Z_{*}),(N_{\varepsilon},N)\}
and
p\in(1,\infty)
.
Then, for functions
v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{p}((0,T)\times G_{\varepsilon})
and
v_{0}\in L^{p}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times G)
the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

The convergence
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}\to v_{0}
holds weakly in
L^{p}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times G)
.

(ii)

The convergence
v_{\varepsilon}\to v_{0}
holds in the two-scale sense in
L^{p}
.

The equivalence above also holds true if weak (two-scale) convergence is replaced by strong (two-scale) convergence.

Proof.

A proof of the result for the weak convergences on bulk domains is given in
[
CDG18
, Proposition 1.19]
and can easily be adapted to thin domains and the lateral boundaries of the channels. We also refer to
[
NJ07
, Proposition 4.7]
) for the case
p=2
and thin channels. For the result regarding the strong convergences, it is used that weak convergence together with convergence of the norms is equivalent to strong convergence in uniformly convex Banach spaces, see
[
Bre11
, Proposition 3.32]
.
∎

Based on the characterisation of two-scale convergence by convergence of the unfolded sequence, the next lemma gives a useful criterion for establishing two-scale convergence of products.

Lemma A.5

(Two-scale convergence of products)
.

Let
p,\,q\in[1,\infty)
and
v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{p}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})
and
w_{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})
. Assuming that

(i)

there exists
v_{0}\in L^{p}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
with
v_{\varepsilon}\to v_{0}
in the (strong) two-scale sense in
L^{p}
,

(ii)

\lVert w_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\smash{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\lesssim 1
and there exists
w_{0}\in L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
with
w_{\varepsilon}\to w_{0}
in the strong two-scale sense in
L^{q}
,

it also holds
v_{\varepsilon}w_{\varepsilon}\to v_{0}w_{0}
in the (strong) two-scale sense in
L^{p}
.

Proof.

We argue similarly as in
[
Wie24
, Lemma 1.16]
, where a similar statement was proven for porous bulk domains, and begin with the case of strong two-scale convergence of
(v_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
. A result for products of multiple sequences is given in
[
RP22
, Proposition 8]
. According to
Lemma A.4
, it suffices to show
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}w_{\varepsilon})\to v_{0}w_{0}
in
L^{p}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
. As
we have
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}\to v_{0}
in
L^{p}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}w_{\varepsilon}\to w_{0}
in
L^{q}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
, it follows
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}w_{\varepsilon})\to v_{0}w_{0}
in
L^{r}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
with
\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}
and, hence, pointwise almost everywhere for a subsequence. From the boundedness of
({\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}w_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
in
L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
and a variant of the dominated convergence theorem (see Pratt’s theorem
[
Pra60
]
in combination with Scheffé’s lemma
[
Rie29
, Section 3]
), we thus obtain
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}w_{\varepsilon})\to v_{0}w_{0}
in
L^{p}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
for a subsequence. As this is valid for any subsequence, the convergence holds true for the whole sequence.

To prove the statement regarding the weak two-scale convergence, we only need to show
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}w_{\varepsilon})\to v_{0}w_{0}
weakly in
L^{p}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
. For every
\varphi\in L^{p^{\prime}}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
, by the dominated convergence theorem and the boundedness of
({\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}w_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
in
L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
, we have
({\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}w_{\varepsilon})\varphi\to w_{0}\varphi
in
L^{p^{\prime}}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
along a subsequence. By the continuity of the dual pairing, we thus deduce

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}w_{\varepsilon})\varphi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}\,(({\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}w_{\varepsilon})\varphi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\to\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{Z_{*}}v_{0}w_{0}\varphi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(A.4)

along a subsequence and conclude as before.
∎

A.2
Strong two-scale convergence

In this section, we present an argument to obtain strong two-scale convergence based on a priori estimates, see
Theorem A.13
below. The following lemma shows why this is sufficient in order to pass to the limit in suitably well-behaved nonlinearities.

Lemma A.6

(Strong two-scale convergence of nonlinearities)
.

Let
p\in[1,2]
and suppose that
g
and
h
satisfy
(D4)
. Then, for every sequence of functions
v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})
which converges to
v_{0}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
strongly in the two-scale sense in
L^{p}
, we have

\displaystyle g(v_{\varepsilon})\to g(v_{0})\qquad\text{strongly in the two-scale sense in $L^{p}$.}

(A.5)

Similarly, if
(v_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
converges to
v_{0}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times N)
strongly in the two-scale sense on
N_{\varepsilon}
in
L^{p}
, we have

\displaystyle h(v_{\varepsilon})\to h(v_{0})\qquad\text{strongly in the two-scale sense on $N_{\varepsilon}$ in $L^{p}$.}

(A.6)

Proof.

The result is an immediate consequence of
Lemma A.4
and the theory of Nemytskii operators (see e.g.
[
Zei90
, Proposition 26.6]
), noting that the application of
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}
commutes with the nonlinear functions
g
and
h
.
∎

Aiming to establish strong two-scale convergence of a sequence of functions
v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon}))
, by
Lemma A.4
it is sufficient to prove strong convergence of the unfolded sequence
({\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
. Due to the low regularity of
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}
with respect to the variable
x^{\prime}\in\Sigma
, we cannot apply a standard Aubin–Lions argument and use a Kolmogorov-type argument for Banach space-valued functions instead, see
[
GN16
]
. For this, the main ingredients are
\varepsilon
-uniform estimates on the time derivatives
\partial_{t}({\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon})
and control of shifts with respect to the
x^{\prime}
–variable, where we follow the reasoning of
[
GN21
]
.
Estimates for the time derivatives are obtained by considering the
L^{2}
-adjoint of
{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}
, see
[
GN21
]
.

Definition A.7

(Averaging operator)
.

The
L^{2}
-adjoint
{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}
of
\varepsilon{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}
is called
averaging operator
, that is
{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}\colon L^{2}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})\to L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})
is a linear and bounded operator such that for all
v_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})
and
\varphi\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
we have

\displaystyle\left({\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}\varphi,v_{\varepsilon}\right)_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}=\varepsilon\left(\varphi,{\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon}\right)_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})}.

(A.7)

By a computation similar to that in
[
CDG18
, Chapter 1.3]
, one obtains (also see
[
GN21
, p. 1590]
)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}\varphi(t,x)=\int_{Y}\varphi\left(t,\varepsilon\left(y+\left\lfloor\tfrac{x^{\prime}}{\varepsilon}\right\rfloor\right),\left(\left\{\tfrac{x^{\prime}}{\varepsilon}\right\},\tfrac{x_{n}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\qquad\text{ for }(t,x)\in(0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon},

(A.8)

but we will not make use of this explicit representation.
From the definition of
{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}
, we directly obtain the following quantitative norm estimate.

Corollary A.8

(Norm of
{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}
,
[
GN21
, Corollary 3.8]
)
.

For all
\varphi\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})
, the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\lVert{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}\varphi\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega^{\mathrm{M}}_{*,\varepsilon})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\smash{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Sigma\times Z_{*})}\raisebox{-0.60275pt}{\mathstrut}}.

(A.9)

The estimates for
\partial_{t}({\mathcal{T}}_{\varepsilon}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{M}})
are then based on the following functional analytic result, which for given functions
u
and an operator
A
acting on
u
